<meta>This page continues the discourse or record associated with Blair, likely part of a larger theological or historical treatise from the 17th century.</meta>

# Blair. 62.

<margin>Blair</margin>

The matter being thus settled, and the mind of the individual being fully persuaded of the truth, it remains for us to consider how this grace operates within the soul. For it is not enough to have a mere historical faith, which even the devils possess <note>original: "fides historica"; a common theological distinction referring to intellectual assent without spiritual transformation</note>, but there must be a living, breathing application of these truths to the heart.

As it is written, "The just shall live by faith" <note>original: "Justus ex fide vivet"; Romans 1:17</note>, so we must understand that this life is not a stagnant thing, but a constant motion toward the Creator. When the soul is once enlightened by the Spirit, the shadows of ignorance flee away, and the will—which was formerly enslaved to its own corrupt desires—is now made free to follow the path of righteousness.

<margin>The Will's Freedom</margin>

We must take great care, however, not to attribute this change to our own natural strength. For as the ancient fathers rightly taught, "Without me you can do nothing" <note>original: "Sine me nihil potestis facere"; John 15:5</note>. It is the primary grace of God that prevents <note>term: "prevents" here means "comes before" or "precedes" (from the Latin *praevenire*), rather than "stops"</note> us, disposing our hearts to desire that which is good, and his subsequent grace that assists us so that we may fulfill those desires.

Therefore, let no man boast in his own works, as if he had contributed anything to his own salvation. For what do you have that you did not receive? And if you received it, why do you boast as if you had not received it? <note>1 Corinthians 4:7</note> All our sufficiency is from God, who has made us able ministers of the New Testament.

<summary>This page discusses the nature of living faith versus mere intellectual belief, emphasizing that spiritual transformation and the freedom of the will are entirely dependent on divine grace rather than human effort.</summary>
<keywords>Blair, faith, divine grace, free will, justification, theology, historical faith</keywords>

# CHARMS OF THE GAELS
<note>original Gaelic: ORTHA NAN GAIDHEAL. While often translated as "Hymns," the word *ortha* specifically refers to a charm, spell, or incantation.</note>

## PRAYERS AND INCANTATIONS
<note>original Gaelic: URNAN AGUS UBAGAN</note>

->WITH LIGHT SHED UPON CUSTOMARY WORDS AND<-
->PRACTICES THAT HAVE PASSED AWAY<-
->COLLECTED FROM ORAL TRADITION<-
->THROUGHOUT THE HIGHLANDS OF SCOTLAND<-
->AND TRANSLATED<-
->FROM GAELIC INTO ENGLISH, BY<-

## ALEXANDER CARMICHAEL

## THE LADIES <note>original: "UIBE" (Old High German). In the context of early Germanic folklore and the Merseburg Charms, this likely refers to the goddesses or divine women who performed healing magic.</note>

# INCANTATIONS

<image-desc>A detailed line drawing of an early medieval Germanic or Anglo-Saxon "great square-headed brooch." The artifact consists of a rectangular head-plate decorated with geometric lines, a central bridge or bow, and a wider foot-plate featuring two large circular lobes. Each lobe contains a cluster of smaller circles, suggesting inlaid gems or repoussé metalwork. Beneath the brooch is a stylized, pointed ornament resembling a spearhead with scrollwork.</image-desc>

--- Page 22 ---
<meta>This page continues the collection of charms and incantations from Section IV, specifically focusing on a ritual for healing skin ailments.</meta>

## THE KNOWLEDGE OF THE ROSE <note>original: "Eolas na Ruaidh." This is a traditional charm for Erysipelas, a bacterial skin infection known as "The Rose" because of its distinctive red inflammation. In Gaelic tradition, "eolas" refers to a healing spell or specialized folk knowledge.</note> [122]

When this charm is applied, the point of a knife or a needle, or the pin <note>original: "tongue"</note> of a brooch or of some other sharp instrument, is pointed threateningly at the affected part. The part is then spat upon and signed with the cross three times in the names <note>referring to the Holy Trinity.</note>

<image-desc>A large decorative initial 'F' in Celtic manuscript style. The letter is constructed from complex interlaced knotwork ribbons and zoomorphic elements. The top left of the 'F' terminates in a beast's head with open jaws, and another similar head is integrated into the lower curve of the letter's stem.</image-desc>

> See, O Son and Christ, <note>original Gaelic: "Faic, a Mhic 's a Chriosda"</note>
> Your Mother's breast is swollen,
> Give rest to the breast,
> Make the swelling wither away;
>     Give rest to the breast,
>     Make the swelling wither away.
>
> See it yourself, O Queen, <note>original Gaelic: "Righinn." Here, the Virgin Mary is addressed as Queen.</note>
> You who gave birth to the Son,
> Put a stop to the [pain of the] breast,
> Put a wasting on the swelling;
>     Put a stop to the breast,
>     Put a wasting on the swelling.
>
> See it yourself, O Jesus,
> You are the King of the elements, <note>original Gaelic: "Righ nan dul." This phrase can also mean "King of creation" or "King of the universe."</note>
> Put a stop to the breast,
> Put a wasting on the udder; <note>original Gaelic: "uth." The use of "breast" and "udder" suggests this charm was used for both nursing mothers and livestock suffering from mastitis.</note>
>     Put a stop to the breast,
>     Put a wasting on the udder.
>
> I see it, said Christ,
> And I will do what is fitting,
> I give rest to the breast,
> And I give peace to the udder;
>     I give rest to the breast,
>     And I give peace to the udder.

<summary>This page describes the ritual and incantation for "The Rose" (Erysipelas), involving the use of a sharp object and an appeal to Christ and the Virgin Mary to heal a swelling.</summary>
<keywords>Eolas na Ruaidh, The Rose, Erysipelas, folk medicine, Gaelic charm, Virgin Mary, mastitis, spiritual healing, Celtic knotwork</keywords>

--- Page 23 ---
<meta>This page continues the instructions for the charm against Erysipelas (The Rose), specifically mentioning its application for both humans and livestock, and concludes with the spoken incantation.</meta>

->INCANTATIONS<-

### CHARM FOR THE ROSE <note>original: "Rose." A historical name for Erysipelas, a skin infection that causes a bright red, painful rash.</note>

...in the names of the three Persons of the Trinity, whether it be the breast of a woman or the udder of a cow. The legend says that Mary and Jesus were walking together when Mary contracted the <term>rose</term> (<term>erysipelas</term>) in her breast, and she said to Jesus:—

> BEHOLD, Son and Christ,
> The breast of Your Mother is swollen,
> Give peace to the breast,
> Subdue the swelling;
> &nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;Give peace to the breast,
> &nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;Subdue the swelling.
> 
> See it yourself, O Queen,
> Since from you the Son was born,
> Soothe the breast,
> Subdue the swelling;
> &nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;Soothe the breast,
> &nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;Subdue the swelling.
> 
> See it, O Jesus,
> Since You are the King of life,
> Soothe the breast,
> Subdue the udder;
> &nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;Soothe the breast,
> &nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;Subdue the udder.
> 
> I see it, said Christ,
> And I do what is right <note>original: "meet." In this context, it means "fitting" or "proper."</note>,
> I give ease to the breast,
> And rest to the udder;
> &nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;I give ease to the breast,
> &nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;And rest to the udder.

# CHARM FOR THE ROSE <note>original: "EOLAS NA RUAIDH." Eolas literally means "knowledge," but in this context refers to a traditional healing charm. "Ruadh" (the Red) is the Gaelic term for the Rose or Erysipelas.</note> <margin>[123]</margin>

**ROSE**, wind-blown, swollen, and deathly,
Leave that side and that place,

# CHARM FOR THE ROSE <note>original: "EOLAS NA RU." Erysipelas was known as "The Rose" due to the bright red, inflamed rash it produced.</note>

O Rose—deadly, death-bringing, swelling,
Leave the udder of the white-spotted cow,
Leave the udder of the cat-headed cow <note>original: "cait-cinn." Likely referring to a cow with specific facial markings or a particular horn shape resembling a cat's ears.</note>,
Leave, leave that swelling,
    And let another swelling come upon you. <note>This reflects a common trope in folk magic where the disease is commanded to transfer itself onto an inanimate object or back onto its own essence.</note>

O stiff, ruddy Rose,
Stubborn in the cow's udder,
Leave the swelling and the udder,
Flee to the foundation of the stone.

I place the Rose against a stone,
I place the stone against the ground,
I place milk in the udder,
I place sap in the corn <note>original: "ar." This refers to ploughed land or the crop growing upon it, representing the return of fertility and health.</note>.

<header>INCANTATIONS</header>

## CHARM FOR THE ROSE <note>Erysipelas, a skin infection characterized by a bright red rash, was commonly called "The Rose" in folk medicine.</note>

You Rose—deathly, deadly, and swollen,
Leave the udder of the white-footed cow,
Leave the udder of the spotted cow,
Leave, leave that swelling,
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;And take yourself to another swelling. <note>This implies transferring the disease from the cow to an inanimate object or back to the source of the ailment.</note>

You Rose—stubborn and obstinate,
Sullen in the udder of the cow,
Leave the swelling and the udder,
Flee to the base of the stone. <note>original: "thrawn." This Scots and Early Modern English term means twisted, crooked, or perversely stubborn.</note>

I place the Rose against the stone,
I place the stone against the earth,
I place milk in the udder,
I place health in the kidney. <note>original: "substance." In this context, it refers to the vital fat or strength of the animal; the kidney was often seen as a seat of vigor.</note>

### CHARM FOR SWELLING OF THE BREAST <note>original: "Eolas at Cioch." This incantation was used to treat mastitis or other swellings in the breasts of women or the udders of livestock.</note>

Do not deny it to a woman,

For his very own sister,
Against "The Rose," against hardness,
Against swelling of the breast.

For the old woman’s <note>original: "carlin"</note> only cow,
Against "The Rose" <note>Erysipelas, a painful bacterial skin infection characterized by redness and swelling.</note>, against hardness, against pain,
Against swelling, against a lump, against a growth,

To his very own sister,
Against "The Rose," against hardness,

<page-num>10</page-num>
<header>UIBE</header>

## CHARM FOR THE TOOTHACHE [126] <note>original: "EOLAS AN DEIDIDH"</note>

**The** teeth of ancient human skeletons found in stone coffins and other burial sites, both within and without enclosures, are usually healthy and complete. This stands in marked contrast to the teeth found in modern human remains, which are generally much decayed if not entirely missing. However, toothache and even artificial teeth must have existed in ancient times, as suggested by Egyptian mummies and the various toothache charms and "toothache wells" found throughout the Highlands. One such charm and one well will serve to illustrate this. The toothache well is located on the island of North Uist. It sits 195 feet above sea level at the foot of a hill 757 feet high, nearly three miles into the moorland from the nearest farm settlement <note>original: "townland"</note>.

The place is called "Cuidh-airidh" <note>literally "shieling fold"; a shieling is a hut or mountain pasture used during the summer</note>, while the well is known by several names, including "Tobar Chuidh-" <note>the Well of the Fold</note>

<image-desc>A circular ornamental medallion featuring a complex Celtic knotwork or interlace pattern. The interlacing bands form a dense, symmetrical weave, and in the center, there appears to be a stylized zoomorphic figure or a tighter knot detail.</image-desc>

> Beautiful Bride <note>St. Bride or Bridget, a central figure in Gaelic folk-Christianity</note> placed <insert>[Or: Before]</insert>
> Before the thumb of the Mother of God,
> On flesh, on flax, on "the rose" <note>original: "chorcraich," referring to erysipelas or a red skin rash</note>,
> On the worm <note>original: "chnoidh"; in traditional folklore, toothache was often believed to be caused by a "tooth-worm" gnawing inside the tooth</note>, on the sharp pain, on the tooth.
>
> The worm that caused me such distress,
> Upon the teeth within my head,
> Tight hell to my tooth,
> A tooth of hell to my agony.

->*  *  *  *  *<-

> A tooth of hell to my distress;
> For as long as I myself shall live,
> May my teeth remain within my head.

### OTHER VARIATIONS—

> On flesh, on gums <note>original: "chir"</note>, on gnawing.
> On sea, on ocean, on coast.
> On color, on beer <note>original: "lionn"; may also refer to a "humor" or fluid in the body</note>, on licking.

<vocab>Charm for the Toothache, Bride, Mother of God, toothache, Shieling fold, Well, Highlands, North Uist</vocab>

<summary>This page discusses the historical prevalence of toothache and describes a specific "toothache well" in North Uist. It also records a protective charm involving St. Bride and the "tooth-worm" to cure dental pain and preserve one's teeth.</summary>
<keywords>toothache, archaeology, North Uist, St. Bride, folk medicine, tooth-worm, Gaelic charms, Cuidh-airidh</keywords>

<page-num>11</page-num>
<header>INCANTATIONS</header>

## TOOTHACHE CHARM

The well is known by several names: <term>airidh</term> <note>the well of the shieling fold; a shieling is a mountain pasture or hut used in summer</note>, <term>Tobar an deididh</term> <note>original Gaelic: "Tobar an deididh"; the well of the toothache</note>, <term>Tobar na cnoidh</term> <note>original Gaelic: "Tobar na cnoidh"; the well of the worm</note>, and <term>Tobar cnuimh fhiacail</term> <note>original Gaelic: "Tobar cnuimh fhiacail"; the well of the tooth-worm</note>. These names arise from the folk belief that toothache is caused by a worm living inside the tooth.

The general name of the spring is <term>Tobar Chuidh-airidh</term> <note>the well of the shieling fold</note>, which distinguishes it from other healing wells throughout the Hebrides. A pilgrim suffering from toothache must not speak, eat, or drink after beginning their journey until they have taken three drinks from the well of Cuidh-airidh. These drinks must be taken in the name of God, in the name of Christ, and in the name of the Holy Spirit.

Some people claim to find no relief, some claim partial relief, and others claim to be completely cured of their toothache after drinking the water from the well of Cuidh-airidh.

<margin>[A prayer]</margin>
> The incantation performed by lovely Bride <note>St. Bridget, often called "the Mary of the Gael"</note>
> Before the thumb of the Mother of God,
> On flax, on herbs <note>original: "wort"</note>, on hemp,
> For the worm, for venom, for the teeth.
> 
> The worm that tortured me,
> In the teeth of my head,
> Hell is right next to my teeth,
> The teeth of hell are distressing me.

->* * * * *<-

> The teeth of hell are close to me;
> As long as I myself shall last,
> May my teeth remain in my head.

### VARIANTS—

> On flax, on a comb, on agony.
> On the sea, on the ocean, on the coast.
> On water, on lakes, on marshes.

<summary>This page describes the ritual and various names for a toothache-healing well on North Uist, alongside an incantation appealing to St. Bride and the Virgin Mary to cure the "tooth-worm."</summary>
<keywords>toothache charm, Tobar Chuidh-airidh, tooth-worm, St. Bride, Hebrides, folk medicine, sacred wells, Gaelic folklore</keywords>

<page-num>12</page-num>
<header>UIBE</header>

### INCANTATION FOR JAUNDICE [127] <note>original title: "EOLAS NA BUDHA"</note>

The following scene was described to me by Angus MacEachain, a herdsman from Staonabrig, South Uist, who was one of the main participants in the event.

The daughter of a farmer in the neighborhood was ill with jaundice. The parish doctor was attending to her, but she was becoming worse instead of better, and her end seemed near. Her distressed parents sent for "Angus of the charms" <note>original Gaelic: "Aonas nan gisrean"; "gisrean" refers to charms, superstitions, or exorcisms</note>, and he came. The man examined the girl and announced that she was possessed by the demon of jaundice, but that he would expel the demon and cure the girl. He requested the mother to build a large fire, the sisters to bring a tub of clear cold water, and the father to bring the plow irons—evil spirits being unable to withstand iron. All of this was promptly done. The exorcist placed the plow irons in the fire, taking great care to ensure they were red-hot. The room was darkened and the patient's eyes were bandaged so that the eyes of the body might be subordinate to the eyes of the mind. Directed by the exorcist, the mother and sisters placed the back of the girl toward the front of the bed and, uncovering it, left the room; the man locked the door after them. Making a clanging noise with the plow irons as if to drive away the jaundice demon, the man replaced the plowshare in the fire and put the coulter <note>the vertical blade of a plow</note> in the water. Then, pretending to take the red-hot plowshare out of the fire, he instead took up the icy-cold coulter and placed it along the spine of the girl.

<image-desc>A large ornamental drop cap 'A' in the Celtic interlace style. The letter is composed of complex knotwork and animal-like shapes, featuring a bird-like head at the top and a similar animalistic head ending the lower left leg. The body of the letter is filled with patterns of small dots.</image-desc>

> For the yellow, for the black, for the kidney-pain, <note>original: "AIR bhuidhe, air dhuibhe, air arnach"</note>
> For the red-disease, for the wasting-disease, <note>original: "Air a ghalar-dhearg, air a ghalar-shearg"</note>
> For the piercing-disease, for the bare-disease, <note>original: "Air a ghalar-tholl, air a ghalar-lom"</note>
> For the brown-disease, for the foot-disease, <note>original: "Air a ghalar-dhonn, air a ghalar-bhonn"</note>
> And for every disease that might <note>original: "’S an gach galar a dh’ fhaodadh"</note>
> Be in the nature of cows <note>original: "A bhi an aorabh ba"</note>
> Or in the shelter of heifers. <note>original: "No an sgath gamhna"</note>

<vocab>Eolas na Budha, Angus Maceachain, Staonabrig, South Uist, Aonas nan gisrean, jaundice, exorcism, Celtic interlace, galar</vocab>

<summary>This page recounts a traditional folk ritual in South Uist used to treat jaundice through the use of "cold iron" and psychological theater. It includes an incantation designed to ward off various diseases in both humans and livestock.</summary>

<keywords>jaundice, folk medicine, South Uist, Angus MacEachain, exorcism, plow irons, Gaelic charms, livestock diseases, Celtic interlace</keywords>

<page-num>13</page-num>
<header>INCANTATIONS</header>

## CHARM FOR JAUNDICE

patient, loudly commanding the demon to depart. The girl screamed in evident agony, calling on the Mother of Christ and on the Foster-mother of Christ <note>Saint Bride (Bridget), who in Gaelic tradition is often called the midwife or foster-mother of Jesus</note>, and on her own mother, to come and rescue her from the brutal treatment of "Black Angus," whom she called the father of evil and the brother of demons. She cried out, imagining that her blood was flowing in streams and her flesh was being burnt off her back, exposing her backbone. While loudly commanding the jaundice demon to depart, the expert exorcist threw the truly red-hot plowshare into the tub of water, adding to the already immense noise in the room. Despite the protests of the father, who said that Angus knew what he was doing, the mother and sisters burst open the door, calling on Mother Mary to rescue the mistreated girl and on Saint Columba <note>original: Calumcille</note> to right her wrongs.

"Whether the cure was due to her simple faith in the exorcist or to the shock to her nervous system, I do not know," continued the narrator, "but in a few days the girl was up and about. She is grateful, but has been shy around me ever since, probably remembering the harsh things she said. She will always believe that I exercised some occult power over the jaundice demon. The case of this girl was as bad as any I have seen. She had been an attractive, beautiful girl with a winning expression and a clear complexion, but she had turned a sickly yellow-black instead of rosy-red."

Angus MacEachain told of this and similar cases with much humor, but without a smile on his lips, though his eyes sparkled and his face glowed with evident enjoyment of the scenes.

> For the jaundice, for the blackleg <term>original: "spaul"; a disease affecting the legs of cattle</term>, for the bloody flux <term>dysentery</term>,
> For the red disease <note>likely "red water," a parasitic disease in cattle</note>, for the withering disease,
> For the bot disease <term>infestation by botfly larvae</term>, for the skin disease,
> For the brown disease, for the foot disease,
> And for every disease that might be
> In the constitution of a cow
> Or clinging to a young bullock <term>original: "stirk"; a yearling heifer or bullock</term>.

# CHARM FOR A SPRAINED SINEW <note>original: Eolas Sgiucha Feithe</note>

<image-desc>A large, ornate Celtic-style initial letter 'R' decorated with intricate interlacing knotwork and a zoomorphic terminal featuring a stylized animal head at the top left.</image-desc>

> A verse that Saint Bride <note>original: ban-naomh Bride; Saint Bridget of Kildare, often called the 'Foster-mother of Christ' in Gaelic tradition</note> made
> For the crippled mariner,
> For the knee, for the ankle, for the hollow of the foot,
> For the nine exhausting diseases, for the three painful diseases,
> Do not refuse it to a beast, do not deny it to a woman.
>
> Christ went upon a horse,
> The horse's leg broke,
> Christ dismounted,
> He made the leg whole.
>
> As Christ healed that,
> May Christ heal this,
> And even more than this,
> If it be His will to do so.
>
> The charm <note>original: an t-eolas</note> that Saint Columba <note>original: Calumcille</note> made,
> On the floor of the glen,
> For a sprained sinew, for a displaced bone—
> You are sick today, you shall be well tomorrow.

<vocab>**Charm** (Eolas), **sprain** (sgiucha), **sinew or vein** (feithe), **Bridget** (Bride), **Christ** (Criosd), **Columba** (Calumcille), **Incantation** (Uibe), **verse or rhyme** (rann)</vocab>

The incantation follows a common folk-healing motif where a divine figure heals an animal, providing a precedent for the current patient's recovery.</summary>

### CHARM FOR A BURSTING VEIN

> The verse <note>original: rune</note> composed by the holy maiden Bride <note>Saint Bridget of Kildare</note>
> For the lame mariner,
> For the knee, for deformity <note>original: crookedness</note>, for lameness <note>original: crippleness</note>,
> For the nine painful diseases, for the three venomous diseases,
> Do not refuse it to a beast, do not deny it to a woman <note>original: dame</note>.
>
> Christ rode on a horse,
> The horse broke its leg,
> Christ dismounted <note>original: went down</note>,
> He healed <note>original: made whole</note> the leg.
>
> Just as Christ healed that,
> May Christ heal this,
> And even more than this,
> If it is His will to do so.
>
> The charm <note>original: eolas</note> composed by Saint Columba <note>Saint Columba (Columcille), the 6th-century Irish abbot and missionary to Scotland</note>,
> On the floor of the valley <note>original: glen</note>,
> For a bursting vein, for a dislocation of a bone—
> You are ill today, you shall be well tomorrow.

<vocab>**Charm** (a healing spell or *eolas*), **Verse** (a rhythmic chant or *rann*), **Bride** (Saint Bridget), **Christ**, **Columba** (the saint of Iona), **bursting vein** (likely a hematoma or severe bruising), **dislocation** (a joint out of place), **incantations** (sacred chants used for folk medicine).</vocab>

<summary>This page presents a traditional Gaelic healing charm used to treat physical injuries like bursting veins and dislocated bones by invoking the spiritual power of Saint Bride, Christ, and Saint Columba. The incantation follows a "sympathetic magic" structure, where Christ's legendary healing of a horse serves as the divine precedent for the patient's own recovery.</summary>
<keywords>Gaelic folklore, healing charms, Saint Bride, Saint Columba, folk medicine, bone-setting, spiritual healing, oral tradition, Scotland</keywords>

### CHARM FOR A SPRAIN <note>original: Eolas an t-Sniamh. "Sniamh" literally means a twist, used here for a sprained or dislocated limb.</note>

<margin>[130]</margin>

<image-desc>A large, ornate decorative initial letter 'T' rendered in a Celtic zoomorphic style. The letter is shaped like a stylized beast with an elongated snout, featuring intricate interlacing knotwork patterns contained within a sharp, triangular frame.</image-desc>

**Bridget** <note>original: Bride; Saint Bridget, often called the "Mary of the Gaels" in Highland tradition.</note> went out
Early in the morning,
With a pair of horses;
A horse broke its leg,

That happened then;
She put bone to bone,
She put flesh to flesh,
She put sinew to sinew,
She put vein to vein,
Just as she healed that,
May I heal this.

### CHARM FOR A SPRAIN <note>original: Eolas an t-Sniamh</note>

**Christ** went to the cross <note>original: ri croich. While this literally means "to the cross," in the context of many similar folk charms, it occasionally alludes to a journey or a hilly place. Here, it sets the scene for a miraculous intervention.</note>,

<meta>This page transitions from the specific text of healing incantations to an explanation of "Fāth-fīth," a specific type of Gaelic magic involving shape-shifting and invisibility, illustrated by the origin story of the legendary hero Ossian.</meta>

<page-num>22</page-num>
<header>UIBE [133]</header>

## FĀTH-FĪTH

"Fāth-fīth" <term>a Gaelic magical spell for invisibility or shape-shifting</term> and "fith-fāth" are interchangeable terms and used without distinction. They are applied to the supernatural power which rendered a person invisible to mortal eyes and which transformed one object into another. Men and women were made invisible, or men were transformed into horses, bulls, or stags, while women were transformed into cats, hares, or female deer <note>original: "hinds"</note>. These transformations <note>original: "transmutations"</note> were sometimes voluntary and sometimes involuntary. The "fith-fath" was especially useful to hunters, warriors, and travelers, rendering them invisible or unrecognizable to enemies and to animals.

Fionn <note>Fionn mac Cumhaill, a legendary Irish and Scottish hunter-warrior</note> had a fairy sweetheart, a daughter of the people of the mounds <note>the *Sídhe*, or fairy folk who lived in ancient burial mounds</note>, but Fionn abandoned her and married a daughter of the sons of men. The fairy was angry at the insult <note>original: "slight"</note> put upon her, and she placed the wife of Fionn under the "fith-fath" spell in the form of a deer of the hill. The wife of Fionn bore a son on the island of Sandray <note>original: "Sanndraigh"</note> in Loch-nan-ceall in Arisaig. The mother possessed so much of the nature of the deer that she licked the forehead <note>original: "temple"</note> of the child when he was born, but she possessed so much of the nature of the woman that she only gave one lick. However, hair like the hair of a fawn grew on the part of the child's forehead which the tongue of the deer-mother had touched. And because of this patch of fawn’s hair on his forehead, the child was called "Ossian" <note>original: "Oisein"</note>, the fawn. 

While still a boy, Ossian followed Fionn and the Feinne <term>the Fianna; Fionn's legendary band of warriors</term> to the hunting-hill to chase the mountain deer. In the midst of the chase, a magic mist darker than night came down upon the hunters, blinding them from one another and from their surroundings—no one knew where anyone else was or where he was himself. A "hunt-wandering" <term>a supernatural state of being lost</term> came over Ossian, and he wandered wearily alone, and at last found himself in a deep green glen surrounded by high blue hills. As he walked along, he saw a timid deer browsing in a green mountain hollow <note>original: "corrie"</note> before him. And Ossian thought to himself that he had never seen a creature so lovely as this timid deer, and he stood gazing upon her with joy. 

But the spirit of the hunt was strong upon Ossian, and the blood of the hunter was hot in his veins, and he drew his spear to throw it at the deer. The deer turned and looked at Ossian and gazed upon him with her full, wistful gray eyes, more lovely and alluring than the blue eyes of love. "Do not hurt me, Ossian," said the deer; "I am your mother under the 'fith-fath,' in the form of a deer abroad and in the form of a woman at home. You are hungry and thirsty and weary. Come home with me, you fawn of my heart." 

And Ossian accompanied the deer step by step until they reached a rock at the base of the hill. The deer opened a panel <note>original: "leaf"</note> in a door in the rock where no door seemed to be, and she went in, and Ossian went in after her. She closed the door-panel in the rock and there was no appearance of a door. And the graceful deer became transformed into a beautiful woman, like the lovely woman of the green gown <note>original: "kirtle"</note> and the locks of gold. There was light in the chamber <note>original: "bower"</note> in the heart of the mountain <note>original: "bosom of the ben"</note> like the light of noontide on midsummer day <note>original: "trath-nona la leth an t-samhraidh"</note>. Nor was it the light of the sun, nor was it the light of the moon, nor was it the light of the—

<summary>This page explains the magical concept of "Fāth-fīth" (shape-shifting and invisibility) and relates the legend of how the hero Ossian was born to a mother transformed into a deer.</summary>
<keywords>Fāth-fīth, shape-shifting, Ossian, Fionn mac Cumhaill, Gaelic folklore, invisibility, Feinne, mythology</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the legend of the hero Ossian and his mother, who was transformed into a deer by a "fith-fath" spell. It describes Ossian's time in a magical dwelling and his eventual return to the world of men.</meta>

<page-num>23</page-num>
<header>INCANTATIONS</header>

# FATH-FITH
<note>original: Fath-Fith; a magical spell used for invisibility or shape-shifting</note>

...star of guidance. His mother prepared food, drink, and music for Ossian. She placed food in a place for him to eat, she placed drink in a place for him to drink, and she provided music in a place for him to hear. Ossian partook of the food, the drink, and the music until he was fully satisfied—his "seven full satiations" <note>original: "seven full satiations"; a traditional Gaelic expression meaning to be completely and perfectly filled</note>. After feasting, Ossian said to his mother, "I am going, mother, to see what Fionn and the Feinne <note>The Feinne, or Fenians, were a legendary band of warrior-hunters led by Fionn mac Cumhaill</note> are doing on the hunting hill." His mother placed her arm around his neck and kissed Ossian with the three kisses of a mother; then she opened the door panel of the bower <note>original: "door-leaf"; a bower is a pleasant, shaded dwelling or a lady's private room</note> and allowed him out. When she closed it, there was no sign of a door in the rock.

Ossian had been feasting on food, drink, and music in the dwelling with his mother for the space of three days, as he thought, but he had actually been in the bower for three years. He composed a song—the first song he ever made—warning his mother to be wary of the men and the hounds of the Feinne.

In his *Book of the Feinne* <note>original: "Leabhar Na Feinne"; a famous 19th-century collection of heroic Gaelic ballads</note>, Iain Campbell of Islay says that he had received fourteen versions of this song of Ossian. Six of these had been sent to him by the present writer. One of these versions was obtained from Effric Mac Iain—also known as Oirig Nic Iain—who was a direct descendant, she said, of Alexander Mac Iain, the chief of the Macdonalds of Glencoe who were killed in the massacre.

Effric Mac Iain was not tall, but she was very beautiful, intelligent, and pleasant. I obtained a silver brooch from her which, she said, had been passed down to her through the generations from the murdered chief of Glencoe. The brooch is made of silver, circular, and beautifully engraved <note>original: "chased"; a technique for decorating metal</note>, though it is much worn.

->**OSSIAN’S WARNING TO HIS MOTHER**<-
<note>original: "'SANAS OISEIN D'A MHATHAIR"</note>

"If you are my mother and you are a deer,
<note>original: "Bheir mi hoirion ho a hau" (and subsequent refrains); these are vocables or rhythmic nonsense syllables used in Gaelic song</note>
Rise before the sun rises on you.

If you are my mother and you are a deer,
Travel the hills before the heat of the hunt.

If you are my mother and you are a deer,
Watch out for the men of the Feinne.

If you are my mother and you are a deer,
Watch out for the hounds of the Feinne.

If you should go to dangerous mountain hollows <note>original: "corries"; circular-shaped valleys in the mountains</note>,
Watch out for the deeds of the hounds—
Hounds of uproar and hounds of rage,
As they engage in the battle-play before you."

It also provides historical context for a specific warning song Ossian composed and describes a brooch belonging to a descendant of the Macdonalds of Glencoe.</summary>

### THE SPELL OF SHAPE-SHIFTING <note>original: FATH fith; a magical incantation used to make oneself invisible or to change one's appearance to avoid detection</note>

I cast this upon you,
By Mary of the augury <note>original: "Muire na frithe"; referring to a traditional Gaelic form of divination or a "frith" (a forest/hunting ground)</note>,
By Bridget of the mantle <note>original: "Bride na brot"; St. Bridget is often associated with a protective cloak</note>,
Against sheep, against ram,
Against kid, against he-goat,
Against fox, and against wolf,
Against sow, and against boar,
Against dog, and against cat,
Against the heavy-hipped bear,
Against the dog of the wilderness,
Against the watchful gaze,
Against cow, against horse,
Against bull, against heifer,
Against daughter, against son,
Against the birds of the air,
Against the crawling things of the earth,
Against the fishes of the sea,
And against the tempests of the gale.

## <term>Fath fith</term> <note>A magical mist or charm used in Highland folklore to make someone invisible or to change their shape, often to protect cattle or hunters from being seen.</note>

I will cast the spell of invisibility on you,
By Mary of the prophecy <note>original: augury</note>,
By Bridget of the coat of mail <note>original: corslet; referring to St. Bridget's protective power</note>,
Against sheep, against ram,
Against goat, against buck,
Against fox, against wolf,
Against sow, against boar,
Against dog, against cat,
Against the heavy-hipped bear,
Against the wild dog of the wilderness,
Against the watchful ant <note>original: 'scan'; likely referring to a small, biting insect or ant</note>,
Against cow, against horse,
Against bull, against heifer,
Against daughter, against son,
Against the birds of the air,
Against the creeping things <note>original: crepidæ; likely used here to mean creatures that crawl or the 'soles' of the earth</note> of the earth,
Against the fishes of the sea,
Against the spirits <note>original: imps</note> of the storm.

<page-num>26</page-num>
<header>UIBE <note>original: Uibe (Gaelic for an incantation or charm)</note></header>

## THE CHARM OF LASTING LIFE <note>original: SIAN A BHEATHA BHUAN</note> [134]

A ‘SIAN’ or ‘seun’ <note>terms for a protective spell or charm</note> is a form of occult agency or supernatural power used to ward off injury and provide invisible protection. Belief in the efficacy of such charms was common, and many stories are told of their success. A woman at Bearnasdale, in Skye, placed such a charm on Macleod of Berneray, Harris, while he was on his way to join Prince Charlie <note>Prince Charles Edward Stuart, "Bonnie Prince Charlie," leader of the 1745 Jacobite Rising</note> in 1745. At the Battle of Culloden, bullets showered upon him like hail, yet they had no effect. When the battle was lost, Macleod threw off his coat to make it easier to flee. His faithful foster-brother, Murdoch Macaskail, followed close behind and picked up the coat. When it was later examined, the coat was found to be riddled with bullet holes. Yet not one of those bullets had harmed Macleod!

Another woman at Bornish, South Uist, placed a charm on Allan Macdonald of Clanranald when he was leaving to join the Earl of Mar at Perth in 1715 <note>The Jacobite Rising of 1715</note>. However, Clanranald forced a young man to join his ranks against the will of the boy's mother, who lived at Staonabrig, South Uist. The woman, a widow, begged Clanranald to leave her only son behind, but he refused. In her anger, she vowed that ‘Ailean Beag’ <note>Little Allan</note>, as Clanranald was called, would never return. She baked two bannocks <note>a type of traditional flatbread</note>—a small one and a large one—and asked her son whether he would rather have the small bannock with his mother’s blessing, or the large one with her curse. The boy chose the small bannock and his mother’s blessing. 

So, she gave him the small bannock and her blessing, along with a crooked sixpence, saying, ‘Here, my son, is a sixpence that has been cursed seven times. Use it in battle against Little Allan and earn your mother’s blessing, or refuse and earn her curse.’ At the Battle of Sheriffmuir, bullets and blows rained down on Allan of Clanranald, but he ignored them; for every blow he received, he gave three in return. When the fighting was at its most intense and the result was still in doubt, the son of the widow of Staonabrig remembered his mother’s instructions. Realizing it was better to fight with her blessing than to fall under her curse, he loaded the crooked sixpence into his gun. He took aim, and Clanranald fell. 

His men crowded around him, weeping and mourning like children. But Glengarry <note>Alastair Ranaldson MacDonell of Glengarry</note> shouted, ‘Today for revenge, tomorrow for sorrow!’ <note>original Scottish Gaelic: "An diugh gu aichbheil am maireach gu bron"</note> and the Macdonalds renewed the fight. Thirsting for revenge, they fell upon the English division of the Duke of Argyll’s army, cutting it to pieces and chasing them for several miles.

<vocab>Sian, seun, Uibe, Bearnasdale, Skye, Macleod of Berneray, Harris, Prince Charlie, Culloden, Murdoch Macaskail, Allan Macdonald of Clanranald, Earl of Mar, Perth, Staonabrig, South Uist, Ailean Beag, Sheriffmuir, Glengarry, Argyll</vocab>

<summary>This page defines the "Sian," a supernatural protective charm, and provides historical anecdotes of its use—and its defeat—during the Jacobite Risings of 1715 and 1745.</summary>
<keywords>Sian, seun, protective charms, Jacobite Risings, Battle of Culloden, Battle of Sheriffmuir, Clanranald, folklore, Scottish Highlands, bannocks, curses</keywords>

<page-num>27</page-num>
<header>INCANTATIONS</header>

## CHARM OF THE LASTING LIFE

When Clanranald’s foster-father was asked whom he mourned and watched over, his only reply was, "He was a man yesterday" <note>original Gaelic: "Bu duine an de e"</note>.

Allan Macdonald of Clanranald was called "Little Allan" <note>original Gaelic: "Ailean Beag"</note>, in contrast to some of his predecessors who had been exceptionally large men. Although he was apparently short in stature, he was exceedingly broad and powerful, active, gallant in his bearing, and greatly beloved by his people.

After the defeat of Viscount Dundee in 1689, Clanranald lived in France for several years. There he made the acquaintance of Penelope, daughter of Colonel Mackenzie, who served as the governor of Tangier under Charles II. Clanranald married Penelope Mackenzie and brought her home. He also brought a French architect, French masons, and French dressed stone <note>original: "freestone"; high-quality stone used for fine architectural work</note> to build a new house at Ormiclate <note>original: "Ormacleit"</note>. The house took seven years to build and was occupied for seven years. On the night of the Battle of Sheriffmuir <note>a major battle of the 1715 Jacobite Rising</note>, when its owner was killed, the house was burned to the ground because the kitchen chimney caught fire. Some days previously, Lady Clanranald had told some guests that she had seen a vision: her eyes melted away in scalding water and her heart burned up like a glowing coal, and she feared some terrible double disaster was to befall her.

"The great ruins of Ormiclate" <note>original Gaelic: "Tota mhor Ormacleit"</note> stand high and picturesque on the monotonous, far-reaching coastal plains <note>original: "machairs"; fertile, low-lying grassy plains found on the coasts of the Hebrides</note> of the Atlantic side of South Uist. The gables are sharply pointed, and because the wings are at right angles to the main building, the ruins appear to great advantage in the long, level landscape.

The dressed stone forming the corners, doors, and windows is of peculiar hardness and of a blue tint.

The farm of Ormiclate had been rented for many years by Mr. John Maclellan, whose wife was Miss Penelope Macdonald, a kinswoman of Flora Macdonald <note>famous for helping Prince Charles Edward Stuart escape after Culloden</note> and of her chief, Clanranald. Mrs. Maclellan was a lady of great beauty, excellence, historical knowledge, and good sense. She had the happiness, a few years before she died, of handing over some jewelry that had been found in the ruins of the castle to her chief and relative, Admiral Sir Reginald Macdonald of Clanranald. The jewelry in all probability had been the property of Penelope Mackenzie, the lady of the gallant Clanranald who died in the uprising of 1715 <note>original: "the ’15"</note>, and for whom Penelope Macdonald had been named.

<vocab>Clanranald, Allan Macdonald, Ailean Beag, Penelope Mackenzie, Ormacleit, Sheriffmuir, South Uist, Flora Macdonald, Admiral Sir Reginald Macdonald</vocab>

<summary>This page recounts the history of Allan Macdonald of Clanranald, his French-educated wife Penelope Mackenzie, and the tragic destruction of their home at Ormiclate on the same night he fell at the Battle of Sheriffmuir.</summary>
<keywords>Clanranald, Jacobite Rising of 1715, Ormiclate, South Uist, Penelope Mackenzie, Highland history, architectural ruins, prophetic visions</keywords>

### THE CHARM OF THE LASTING LIFE <note>original Gaelic: "Sian a Bheatha Bhuan"</note>

<image-desc>A large, decorative Celtic zoomorphic initial 'C' featuring two intertwined dragon-like creatures. Their elongated bodies are filled with complex knotwork patterns. One creature's head is at the top of the 'C', biting the neck of the second creature, whose body curves around to form the bottom half of the letter. The design is characteristic of the Insular art style.</image-desc>

I place the charm <note>original: "seun"</note> upon your body,
And upon your destiny,
The charm of the God of the elements <note>original: "Dhe nan dul"</note>
For your protection.

The charm that Bride <note>St. Brigid, often called "Bride of the cattle" in Gaelic tradition</note> of the flocks placed
Around the smooth neck of White-hand <note>original: "Dhorn-ghil"; likely a poetic name for a calf or a beloved person</note>,
The charm that Mary placed around her Son,
Between sole and throat,
Between breast and knee,
Between back and chest,
Between shoulder and sole,
Between eye and hair.

The host of Michael <note>St. Michael the Archangel, the celestial warrior</note> at your side,
The shield of Michael on your shoulder,
There is nothing between heaven and earth
That can overcome the King of grace.

No blade shall tear you,
No sea shall harm you,
No woman shall enchant you <note>original: "cha teum mnai thu"; literally "no women shall bite/sting you," likely referring to the evil eye or witchcraft</note>,
No man shall overpower you.

The mantle of Christ himself around you,
The shadow of Christ himself over you,
From the crown of your head
To the soles of your feet.

<header>INCANTATION <note>original: "UIBE"</note></header>

<image-desc>A large decorative Celtic-style ornament on the left side of the upper text. The illustration features intricate knotwork within a triangular frame, terminating in a stylized zoomorphic head, possibly a bird or a serpent.</image-desc>

<margin>[135]</margin>
### THE CHARM OF THE LASTING LIFE <note>original: "Sian a Bheatha Bhuan"</note>

I prepare the charm of the lasting life,
For your cattle, swift, broad-backed, and full;
On the little rock where the herd <note>original: "spreidh," meaning livestock or cattle</note> lies down,
May they rise up healthy and whole.

Downward with victory and with blessing,
Upward with speed and with companionship,
Without a frown, without envy, without jealousy,
Without the "small eye," without the "big eye,"
Without the eye of the five who neglect <note>The "Evil Eye" was often attributed to specific numbers of people or the unintentional neglect of social rituals</note>.

I drain this away, a draining of the vein of envy
From the head of the house and from the families of the townland;
May every bad result and every bad influence
That was destined for you, fall upon them instead <note>A common feature in Gaelic charms where a curse is "turned" and sent back to the one who originated it</note>.

If a tongue has cursed you,
A heart has blessed you;
If an eye has wounded you,
A good intention has made you fortunate.

Turning and twisting,
Hard holly and prickly shrubs,
Against their ewes and their bull-calves,
For the length of nine and nine-score years <note>Nine and 180 years; "nine" is a sacred number in Celtic tradition, often used to denote a complete or eternal cycle</note>.

<gloss>Charm of the Lasting Life, Incantation, cattle, herd/livestock, blessing, envy, family/household, nine</gloss>

## THE CHARM OF THE LASTING LIFE <note>original: "Sian a Bheatha Bhuan"</note>

I WILL place the charm of the lasting life
Upon your cattle—active, broad-backed, and well-fed—
The small hill upon which the herds shall lie down,
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;That they may rise from it healthy and whole.

Down with success and with blessing,
Up with activity and companionship <note>original: "following," likely referring to the herd staying together or the presence of protective spirits</note>,
Without envy, without malice, without ill-will,
Without the "small eye," without the "large eye,"
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;Without the five eyes of neglect <note>The "five eyes" refers to a folk belief about different types of gazes—such as those of a jealous neighbor or a stranger—that could unintentionally blight livestock</note>.

I will drain this away, the drawing out of the envious vein
From the head of the house and the families of the townland <note>term: "townland" - a traditional Gaelic land division</note>,
So that every evil trait and every evil influence
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;Inherent in you shall stick to them instead.

&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;If a tongue cursed you,
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;A heart blessed you;
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;If an eye blighted you,
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;A good wish made you prosper.

A commotion and a turning upside-down,
A harsh beating with holly and a pale withering
To their female sheep and to their male calves,
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;For the nine and the nine-score years <note>9 and 180 years; "nine" is a significant number in Gaelic numerology, often representing a very long time or eternity</note>.

<image-desc>A large ornamental drop-cap 'S' rendered in a traditional Celtic knotwork style. The body of the letter consists of interlacing ribbons that terminate in stylized zoomorphic heads at the top and middle. A triquetra knot is nested within the lower curve of the 'S'.</image-desc>

### THE CHARM OF BRIDE <note>original: "SIAN BRIDE"</note>

> THE charm that Bride of the victories put,
> Around my sheep, around my goats, around my cattle,
> Around my horses, around my prize-beasts, around my burden-beasts, <gloss>camel/beast of burden</gloss> <note>original: "cathmhil" (war-warrior) and "cual/camhal" (bundle/camel). These terms were often used poetically in charms to encompass all forms of valuable livestock.</note>
> Early and late, going home and away.

> Keeping them from rocks, from cliffs,
> From the hooves and the horns of one another,
> From the birds of the Red Rock,
> And from Luath of the Fenians. <note>Luath (meaning "Swift") was the legendary hound of the hero Fionn mac Cumhaill in Gaelic mythology.</note>

> From the grey falcon of the Rock of Sorrow, <note>original: "Creag Duilionn"</note>
> From the brindled eagle of the High Mountain, <note>original: "Beinn-Ard"</note>
> From the swift hawk of the Fort-Mound, <note>original: "Torr-an-Duin"</note>
> And the stubborn raven of the Bard's Rock. <note>original: "Creag-a-Bhaird"</note>

> From the wily red fox,
> From the wolf of the mountain pass, <note>original: "mada-ulai a Mhaim"</note>
> From the tufted, long-bodied marten,
> And from the unsteady, heavy-hipped bear. <note>original: "maghan" – Bear. Though bears were extinct in Scotland by this time, the word survived in oral charms as a traditional predator.</note>

---

> From every four-footed, clawed creature,
> And every winged hatchling.

## THE CHARM OF ST. BRIDE

<margin>beast of burden <note>original: "camel"</note></margin>
The charm placed by Bride the beneficent,
On her goats, on her sheep, on her cattle, <note>original: "kine"</note>
On her horses, on her war-steeds, on her herds,
Early and late, coming home and leaving home.

To keep them from rocks and ridges,
From each other's hooves and horns,
From the predatory birds of the Red Rock,
And from Luath of the Fenians. <note>Luath, meaning "Swift," was the legendary hound of the hero Fionn mac Cumhaill in Gaelic mythology.</note>

From the blue peregrine hawk of the Rock of Sorrow, <note>original: "Creag Duilion"</note>
From the brindled eagle of the High Mountain, <note>original: "Ben-Ard"</note>
From the swift hawk of the Mound Fort, <note>original: "Tordun"</note>
From the surly raven of the Poet’s Rock. <note>original: "Bard’s Creag"</note>

From the cunning fox, <note>original: "fox of the wiles"</note>
From the wolf of the mountain pass, <note>original: "the Mam"</note>
From the foul-smelling polecat, <note>original: "fumart"</note>
And from the bear with the unsteady hip.

->\* &nbsp; &nbsp; &nbsp; &nbsp; &nbsp; \* &nbsp; &nbsp; &nbsp; &nbsp; &nbsp; \* &nbsp; &nbsp; &nbsp; &nbsp; &nbsp; \* &nbsp; &nbsp; &nbsp; &nbsp; &nbsp; \*<-
->\* &nbsp; &nbsp; &nbsp; &nbsp; &nbsp; \* &nbsp; &nbsp; &nbsp; &nbsp; &nbsp; \* &nbsp; &nbsp; &nbsp; &nbsp; &nbsp; \* &nbsp; &nbsp; &nbsp; &nbsp; &nbsp; \*<-

From every hoofed creature with four feet,
And from every hatched creature with two wings.

<term>St. Bride</term>: St. Bridget of Kildare, often called the "Mary of the Gael," associated with fertility and livestock. 
<term>Kine</term>: An archaic plural for cows or cattle. 
<term>Chargers</term>: Powerful horses used in battle or for heavy work. 
<term>Fenians</term>: <note>original: "Feinne"</note> The legendary band of warriors led by Fionn mac Cumhaill. 
<term>Fumart</term>: Also spelled "foumart," a traditional name for a polecat, known for its strong scent. 
<term>Mam</term>: A Gaelic term for a large, rounded hill or a mountain pass.

### A LOVE CHARM <margin>[138]</margin> <note>original: EOLAS GRADHAICH</note>

The people quote many proverbs relating to love and to love charms. "Lunacy and love are twins." "The sickness of love and the sickness of madness are one and the same." "Love shuts a thousand eyes but wakes—"

<image-desc>A large, ornate Celtic knotwork initial 'C' containing zoomorphic details, including a beast's head at the top terminal and a tail-like coil at the bottom. The interior of the letter is filled with dense, interlacing ribbon patterns.</image-desc>

->IT is no love charm for you<-
->To draw water through a straw,<-
->But the love of the man [woman] who comes to you,<-
->To draw his [her] warmth over you.<-

->Rise early on the Sunday,<-
->To a flat, level flagstone,<-
->Take with you a priest’s hood <note>original: *currachd sagart*; this may refer to a literal hood or the folk name for a plant like wood-sorrel</note>,<-
->And a peaked tent <note>original: *puball beannach*; likely a folk name for the buttercup</note>.<-

->Lift that upon your shoulder<-
->In a wooden shovel,<-
->Get nine stalks of bracken <note>original: *roinnich*</note><-
->Cut with an axe,<-

->Three bones of an old man,<-
->Taken from a grave,<-
->Burn them on a fire of dry brushwood,<-
->And turn them all into ashes.<-

->Shake [the ashes] on the very breast of your lover<-
->Against the sting of the north wind,<-
->And I will be your surety and your warrant,<-
->That this man [woman] will not leave you.<-

## LOVE CHARM

*Còig mìle farmaid* <note>original: cuig mile farmaid</note>—“Love will close a thousand eyes but waken five thousand jealousies.”

The "lucky bones" are the joint of the big toe of the right foot and the nail joints of the left foot of an old man. These are said to be the first parts of the human body to decay.

> ->To draw their warmth toward you.<-
>

>

> ->Against the sting of the north wind;<-
> ->And I will vow and guarantee to you,<-
> ->That this man [or woman] will never leave you.<-

### LOVE CHARM

*An ornate, Celtic-style illuminated letter 'E' begins the text.*

A charm of love for you, <note>original: EOLAS gradhaidh dut</note>
->To draw water through a straw,<- <note>original: Uisge thraghadh thromh shop; a metaphor for an impossible task made possible by magic</note>
->The warmth of the man [or woman] who suits you,<-
->To draw their love toward you.<-

Arise early on Sunday, <note>original: Di-domhnaich</note>
->To a level flagstone by the shore;<-
Take with you the "peaked canopy" <gloss>butterbur</gloss>, <note>original: beannach pubaill</note>
->And the "priest's cap" <gloss>foxglove</gloss>.<- <note>original: currachd sagairt</note>

A little pinch of embers
->In the hem of your garment,<-
A choice handful of "sea-tangle" <note>original: gruaigean; likely a type of seaweed or lichen used in folk medicine</note>,
->In a wooden shovel.<-

Three bones of an old man,
->After they have been taken from a grave,<-
Nine stalks of "royal fern," <note>original: reann-roinnich</note>
->After they have been cut with an axe.<-

Burn them on a fire of dry brushwood
->And turn them all into ashes;<-
Scatter them on the breast of your lover's garment,
->Against the sting of the north wind.<-

Go and pursue the success of the spell,
->The turn of the five circuits,<-
And I give you word and warrant
->That man [or woman] will never leave you.<-

<meta>This page continues the exploration of traditional Gaelic folk magic and healing rituals. While the previous page focused on a complex love charm involving botanical and symbolic elements, this page shifts to a protective ritual and incantation used to counteract the "evil eye," a common supernatural concern in Highland folklore.</meta>

### THE INJURY OF THE EYE
<note>original: "CRONACHDUINN SUIL"</note>

The results of the evil eye appear in yawning, vomiting, and a general disturbance of the body's system. The face assumes an appearance that is grim, gruesome, and repulsive—‘greann, greisne, grannda’ <note>original Gaelic: "greann, greisne, grannda"; these terms describe a surly, shuddering, and ugly countenance</note>.

This formula for removing the effects of the evil eye is passed down from male to female, or from female to male, and it is effective <note>original: "efficacious"</note> only when transmitted in this alternating way. Before reciting it over the sick person or animal, the healer <note>original: "operator"</note> goes to a stream where both the living and the dead pass <note>This refers to a stream crossed by a bridge or ford that is part of a traditional funeral route to a graveyard</note> and scoops up water into a wooden ladle in the name of the Holy Trinity. A metal ladle is never used. Upon returning, a married woman’s gold ring, a piece of gold, a piece of silver, and a piece of copper are placed in the ladle. The sign of the holy cross is then made, and this rhyme is repeated in a slow, chanting <note>original: "recitative"</note> manner—the name of the person or animal under treatment being mentioned toward the end. In the case of an animal, a woollen thread, usually of the natural color of the sheep, is also used.

<image-desc>A circular Celtic knotwork illustration featuring an intricate, spiraling woven pattern that creates a sense of depth and movement. The design includes a decorative outer border with a zoomorphic flourish at the top, consistent with traditional Gaelic manuscript art.</image-desc>

> Who will turn back the injury of the eye? <note>original: "O a thilleas cronachduinn suil?"</note>
> I will turn it back, I hope, <note>original: "Tillidh mise tha mi’n duil,"</note>
> In the name of the King of the elements. <note>original: "Ann an ainm Righ nan dul"; "King of the elements" is a traditional Gaelic title for God</note>
> Three times seven proclamations so right, <note>original: "Tri seachd gairmeachdain co ceart,"</note>
> Christ spoke at the door of the city; <note>original: "Labhair Criosd an dorusd na cathrach"</note>
>   Mary’s Prayer one, <note>original: "Paidir Moire a h-aon"</note>
>   The King’s Prayer two, <note>original: "Paidir Righ a dha"</note>
>   Mary’s Prayer three,
>   The King’s Prayer four,
>   Mary’s Prayer five,
>   The King’s Prayer six,
>   Mary’s Prayer seven;
> Seven prayers of Mary shall turn back
> The injury of the eye,
> Whether it be upon a person or upon a beast, <note>original: "bruid"; meaning brute or livestock</note>
> Upon a horse or upon a cow; <note>original: "marc no air earc"</note>
> May you be in complete health tonight,
> ->[*The name of the person or animal*]<-
> ->In the name of the Father, the Son, and the Holy Spirit. Amen.<-

<vocab>evil eye, cronachduinn suil, Scottish Gaelic charm, Carmina Gadelica, Highland folklore, prayer, silver, gold, copper, Holy Trinity, Alexander Carmichael, Uibe, Paidir Moire</vocab>

<summary>This page describes the symptoms of the "evil eye" and the specific ritual and Gaelic incantation required to heal those afflicted. The process involves collecting water from a funeral route and reciting a rhythmic prayer that invokes Christ and Mary.</summary>
<keywords>evil eye, folk medicine, Gaelic incantations, ritual healing, Alexander Carmichael, Scottish Highlands, Paidir Moire, livestock protection</keywords>

<page-num>43</page-num>
<header>INCANTATIONS</header>

## THWARTING THE EVIL EYE

The woollen thread is tied around the tail. The consecrated water is then given as a drink <note>original: "draught"</note> and sprinkled over the head and backbone. In the case of a cow, the horns and the space between the horns are carefully anointed.

The remnant of the water, not a single drop of which must have touched the ground previously, is poured over a corner stone, a threshold flagstone, or another immovable stone or rock, which is said to split if the sickness is severe. Experts claim to distinguish whether it was a man or a woman who cast the evil eye: if a man, the copper adheres to the bottom of the upturned ladle, representing the <term>‘many turns’</term> <note>original Gaelic: "iomadh car"</note> or twists in a man’s dark, wily heart; if a woman, only the silver and gold adhere. In this ritual, the heart of a woman compared to that of a man is not—as the poet might say—"as moonlight unto sunlight and as water unto wine," but rather as gold and silver are to copper and brass. Old women in the Highlands say that if men’s hearts were laid bare, they would be found to contain many more twists, turns, and wiles than those of women.

> ->WHO shall thwart the evil eye?<-
> ->I shall thwart it, I think,<-
> ->In the name of the King of life.<-
> ->Three times seven potent commands,<-
> ->Christ spoke <note>original: "spake"</note> in the gateway of the city;<-
> ->Father <note>original Latin: "Pater"</note> Mary one,<-
> ->Father King two,<-
> ->Father Mary three,<-
> ->Father King four,<-
> ->Father Mary five,<-
> ->Father King six,<-
> ->Father Mary seven;<-
> ->Seven Father Maries will thwart<-
> ->The evil eye,<-
> ->Whether it be on man or on beast,<-
> ->On horse or on cow;<-
> ->May you be in your full health this night,<-
> ->[*The name of the sufferer*]<-
> ->In the name of the Father, the Son, and the Holy Spirit. Amen.<-

<vocab>evil eye, incantation, Highlands, iomadh car, Pater Mary, King of life</vocab>

<summary>This page details the conclusion of a ritual to cure the "evil eye," including the symbolic use of metals to identify the gender of the curser and a rhythmic incantation invoking Christ and the Virgin Mary.</summary>
<keywords>folk medicine, Scottish Highlands, evil eye, protective charms, gender symbolism, Pater Mary, Gaelic folklore</keywords>

<header>UIST</header>

## INCANTATION FOR THE EYE-INJURY [141] <note>original: "Eolas a Bheum Shula"</note>

<image-desc>A large, ornate decorative initial 'S' in the Celtic style. The letter is formed by the body of a long-necked, winged serpent or dragon-like creature, with its tail and body coiling to form the loops of the 'S'. The interior of the creature's body is filled with intricate interlaced knotwork.</image-desc>

I TRAMPLE <note>original: "Saltraim"</note> upon the eye
As a duck tramples on a pool,
As a swan tramples on water,
As a horse tramples on the earth,
<margin>[heifer-hoof-marks</margin> As a heifer <note>original: "earc"</note> tramples on a corner,
<margin>[The Being</margin> As the host of the elements tramples,
    As the host of the elements tramples.

I have the power of the wind over it,
I have the power of the heather over it,
I have the power of the fire over it,
I have the power of the thunder over it,
I have the power of the lightning over it,
I have the power of the storm over it,
    I have the power of the moon <note>original: "gile"</note> over it,
    I have the power of the sun over it,
    I have the power of the stars over it,
    I have the power of the skies over it,
    I have the power of the heavens
        And of the earth <note>original: "ce"</note> over it,
    Power of the heavens
        And of the earth over it.

A third of it on the grey stones,
A third of it on the steep mountains,
A third of it on the rapid waterfalls,

<note>This charm utilizes a rhythmic "trampling" metaphor to strip the evil eye of its power, transferring the perceived illness or curse away from the victim and into the natural elements of the earth.</note>

<summary>This page contains a rhythmic Gaelic incantation designed to heal an "eye-injury" or "evil eye" by invoking the elemental powers of nature and celestial bodies. The speaker claims authority over the curse, symbolically "trampling" it and dispersing its harmful energy into stones, mountains, and waterfalls.</summary>
<keywords>evil eye, Gaelic charm, elemental magic, incantation, Uist, folklore, spiritual healing, Scottish Highlands</keywords>

## EXORCISM OF THE EYE

I trample upon the eye,
As the duck tramples upon the lake,
As the swan tramples upon the water,
As the horse tramples upon the plain,
<margin>Caledonian cattle</margin>
As the cow tramples upon the ‘iuc,’ <note>original: "iuc" — an obscure term likely referring to a corner, a hollow, or a narrow pass in the hills</note>
<margin>The Being</margin>
As tramples the host of the elements, <note>The "host of the elements" refers to the spiritual powers or celestial forces governing the natural world.</note>
->As tramples the host of the elements.<-

I have power of the wind over it,

I have power of thunder over it,
I have power of lightning over it,

->I have power of the heavens<-
->And of the worlds over it.<-

<margin>A third</margin>
A portion of it upon the grey stones,
A portion of it upon the steep hills,
A portion of it upon the rapid waterfalls,

<note>This concluding section of the incantation seeks to transfer the "evil eye" from the victim into the inanimate landscape—stones, hills, and water—where its power can be neutralized by the much greater forces of the natural and celestial worlds.</note>

<term>Exorcism</term>: A ritual or prayer intended to drive out an evil influence or curse.
<term>Elements</term>: The fundamental forces of nature (wind, fire, etc.).
<term>Firmament</term>: An archaic term for the vault of the sky.
<term>Caledonian cattle</term>: A reference to the ancient, hardy breeds of cattle found in Scotland.
<term>Iuc</term>: A specific, likely topographical, term for a cattle-path or corner.

<summary>This page concludes the "Exorcism of the Eye" incantation, using a rhythmic series of "trampling" metaphors and natural invocations to banish a curse into the landscape. It emphasizes the speaker's spiritual authority over the elements and celestial bodies to protect the victim.</summary>
<keywords>incantation, evil eye, Gaelic folklore, elements, celestial power, Scottish Highlands, protection ritual</keywords>

<term>Incantations</term>: Ritual chants or prayers (often called *eòlais* in Gaelic) used for healing or protection.

### THE EYE INCANTATION <margin>[142]</margin>

<image-desc>A large, elaborate Celtic-style illuminated initial 'C'. The letter is composed of intricate knotwork and features three stylized zoomorphic heads—likely serpents or dragons—with open maws and sharp teeth, intertwined within the structure of the letter.</image-desc>An eye has observed you,
->A mouth has spoken of you,<-
->A heart has meditated upon you,<-
->A desire has envied you.<- <note>original: "Smunaich miann thu" — refers to the internal act of coveting or wishing ill upon another.</note>

Four have done you harm:
A man and a woman,
A youth and a maiden; <note>original: "Mac agus murn" — literally "a son and a girl/damsel."</note>
Three I set against them to restrain them:
->The Father,<-
->The Son,<-
->The Holy Spirit.<-

I call Mary to witness,
The aiding Mother of the people;
I call Bride to witness,
The foster-mother of Christ of the virtues; <note>original: "Muime Chriosda nam buadh." In Gaelic tradition, St. Bridget (Bride) is affectionately known as the foster-mother of Jesus.</note>
I call Columba to witness,
Apostle of the shore and the sea; <note>original: "Chaluim." St. Columba (Colum Cille), the patron saint of the Hebrides and the Irish missionary who founded Iona.</note>
And I call Heaven to witness,
And every saint and every angel who is above.

If it was a man who did you harm,
With an evil eye,
With an evil intent,
With an evil purpose, <note>original: "ruam" — can mean a flush of anger or a malicious look.</note>

<term>Eye Incantation</term>: Known as *Cronachdain Sùil*, this is a protective prayer or "charm" meant to heal someone believed to be suffering from the "Evil Eye" (malice or envy from others).
<term>Charm</term>: <note>original: "Uibe"</note> A specific type of ritual chant or spoken spell used for healing or warding off evil.
<term>Mary</term>: <note>original: "Moire"</note> The Virgin Mary, invoked here as a compassionate helper and protector.
<term>Bride</term>: St. Bridget of Kildare, a central figure in Celtic Christianity often associated with midwifery, protection, and the hearth.
<term>Columba</term>: The famous 6th-century saint of Iona, particularly revered by seafaring communities.
<term>Holy Spirit</term>: <note>original: "Spiorad Numh"</note> An archaic spelling of *Spiorad Naomh*.

<summary>This page contains an incantation used to protect against the "Evil Eye," identifying the sources of harm—such as envy and ill-will—and calling upon the Holy Trinity and prominent saints like Mary, Bride, and Columba to act as witnesses and healers.</summary>
<keywords>Evil Eye, Mary, Bride, Columba, Holy Trinity, Gaelic charms, folk religion, protection ritual, spiritual healing</keywords>

INCANTATIONS

# COUNTERACTING THE EVIL EYE

->An eye has looked upon you,<- <note>original: "covered thee" — in this context, it implies casting a shadow or a malicious gaze over someone.</note>
->A mouth has spoken of you,<-
->A heart has envied you,<-
->A mind has desired you.<-

->Four have caused your affliction,<- <note>original: "made thee thy cross" — "cross" is used here to mean a burden, trial, or suffering.</note>
->A man and a wife,<-
->A youth and a maiden;<-
->Three will I send to stop them,<-
->The Father,<-
->The Son,<-
->The Holy Spirit.<-

->I call upon Mary,<-
->The helpful Mother of humanity,<-
->I call upon Bride,<- <note>St. Bride (or Brigid) is a major figure in Celtic Christianity and folklore, often called the "Foster-mother of Christ."</note>
->Foster-mother of Christ the Almighty,<-
->I call upon Columba,<- <note>St. Columba (Colum Cille) was the Irish missionary who founded the monastery at Iona and is a patron saint of the Highlands and Islands.</note>
->Apostle of the shore and the sea,<-
->And I call upon heaven,<-
->To all the saints and angels who are above.<-

->If it is a man who has done you harm,<-
->With an evil eye,<-
->With an evil wish,<-
->With an evil passion,<-

<term>Evil eye</term>: A widespread folk belief that certain individuals can cause injury or bad luck to others through a gaze fueled by envy or malice.
<term>Bride</term>: The Gaelic name for Saint Brigid, who occupies a unique position in folklore as a protector of women, children, and cattle.
<term>Columba</term>: A reference to Saint Columba, whose protection was often invoked by those living near the sea or traveling.
<term>Incantation</term>: A spoken formula or prayer believed to have magical or healing power.
<term>Folk medicine</term>: Traditional healing practices that combine herbal remedies, prayers, and rituals.

<page-num>50</page-num>

# INCANTATION
<note>original: "UIBE"</note>

May you cast off every evil, <note>original: "Gun tilg thu diot gach olc"</note>
Every frown, <note>original: "mug"</note>
Every scowl, <note>original: "gnug"</note>
Every gloom, <note>original: "gruam"</note>
And may you be well forever,
By the power of this thread <note>original: "snathle" — a ritual thread or cord used in Gaelic folk magic to bind protection or "tie" a blessing to a person.</note>
As it goes around you, <note>original: "dhol a d' dhail mu'n cuart" — literally "going to meet you all around"; this refers to the physical act of winding the thread around the sufferer's wrist or neck.</note>
In the honor of God and Jesus,
And the Spirit of eternal healing. <note>original: "Spioraid ioc bhi-bhuain"</note>

***

<term>Uibe</term>: A Gaelic term for an incantation, charm, or ritual spell, specifically one used for healing or protection from supernatural harm.
<term>Olc</term>: Meaning "evil" or "harm," specifically referring here to the physical or spiritual sickness caused by the "Evil Eye."
<term>Mug, Gnug, Gruam</term>: These three words describe various states of sullenness, scowling, or gloominess. In this context, they represent the negative symptoms or "cloud" cast over a person by a malicious gaze.
<term>Snathle</term>: A sacred thread. In Highland tradition, a thread (often of red wool) was prepared with specific prayers and tied around a person or animal to protect them from envy and illness.
<term>Ios</term>: The Gaelic form of the name Jesus.
<term>Ioc</term>: A word for healing, balm, or a remedy.
<term>Bhi-bhuain</term>: Eternal, everlasting, or ever-living.

<summary>This page provides the concluding lines of a Gaelic protective incantation, focusing on casting off the negative symptoms of the Evil Eye and using a ritual thread for healing. It invokes the Holy Trinity to ensure the person remains well indefinitely.</summary>

<term>Harassment</term>: Within the context of folk belief, this refers to the spiritual or physical distress and exhaustion believed to be inflicted by a curse or the "Evil Eye."

## A CHARM FOR THE EYE

> <image-desc>A large, ornate decorative initial 'U' in a Celtic or Insular style. The letter is composed of intricate interlaced knotwork and terminates at the top in two zoomorphic heads facing outward.</image-desc>A bright charm <note>original: "Uibe"; a healing spell or amulet</note> sent by gentle Mary <note>The Virgin Mary</note>,
> From across the stream, across the sea, and across the land,
> <margin>cat</margin>Against the power and the cat-like glance of envy,
> Against the son of arms <note>possibly a warrior or one bearing ill intent</note>,
> Against the tooth of the short-dog <note>original: "coin-ghiorr"; likely a wolf or a specific breed of hound</note>,
> Against the swelling of the short-dog,
> Against three obstructions of the heart,
> Against three obstructions of the bone,
> Against three obstructions of the wasting sickness,
> And against the film upon the mare's eye.
>
> Whoever it was that cast the [Evil] Eye on you,
> May it lie upon themselves,

> I will turn the eye,

> And the tongue of death in the entire boundary.
>
> Three small, kind maidens,
> Who were born on the same night as Christ;
> If those three are alive tonight,
> May you be alive, poor creature.

## SPELL FOR THE EVIL EYE

The beautiful charm that gentle Mary sent,
Over stream, over sea, over land,
<margin>cat</margin>Against enchantments, against the withering glance,
Against hostile <note>original: "inimical"</note> power,
Against the teeth of the wolf,
Against the strength <note>original: "testicles"</note> of the wolf,
Against the three crooked cranes,
Against the three crooked bones,
Against the three crooked wasting-sicknesses <note>original: "creothail"</note>,
And against the film <note>original: "leothair"</note> of the ground.

Whoever cast the [Evil] Eye on you,
May it fall back upon himself,
May it fall upon his house,
May it fall upon his flocks,
May it fall upon his wealth <note>original: "substance"</note>,
May it fall upon his health <note>original: "fatness"</note>,
May it fall upon his means,
May it fall upon his children,
May it fall upon his wife,
May it fall upon his descendants.

I will subdue the eye,
I will suppress the eye,
And I will banish the eye,
The three inviting arteries (?),
And the tongue of death completely.
Three lovely little maidens,
Who were born on the same night as Christ,
If these three are alive tonight,
May life be near you, poor creature <note>original: "beast"</note>.

# INCANTATION FOR THE EYE
<note>original: "OBI RI SHUIL"</note>

<margin>[144]</margin>

### CHARM FOR THE EVIL EYE
<note>original: "Eoir Beum Sula"</note>

<image-desc>A large, decorative initial letter 'S' in the Celtic Insular style. The letter is zoomorphic, depicting a dragon-like creature with a bird's head. Whoever it was who cast the [evil] eye on you,

The part of her that the ravens do not eat,
May the [smaller] birds eat.

Over the tops of the bushes,
May he likewise lift from you now
Every ailment <note>original: "cnid"</note>, every envy, and every jealousy,
From this day until the final day of your life.

<meta>This page continues the collection of Gaelic incantations, specifically focusing on a variation of the "Beum Sula" (Stroke of the Eye) or Evil Eye charm. <page-num>57</page-num>
<header>INCANTATIONS</header>

### CHARM FOR THE EVIL EYE

Whoever cast the [evil] eye upon you,
May it fall back upon himself,
May it fall upon his house,
May it fall upon his livestock,
On the shuffling <term>hag</term> <note>original: "carlin," a Scots or Northern English term for an old woman, often used to imply a crone or a witch</note>,
On the sour-faced <term>hag</term>,
On the leaping <term>hag</term>,
On the thin-legged <term>hag</term>,
Who rose in the morning,
With her eye fixed on her livestock,
With her livestock in her "luck" <note>original: "seoin," a Gaelic term likely referring to a state of luck, magical influence, or a protective aura</note>,
May she never own a cattle-fold,
May she never have even half of her desires;
The part of her which the ravens do not eat,
May the [other] birds devour.

Four people cast the [evil] eye upon you:
A man and a lady, a youth and a maiden;
Three who will cast the envy away from you:
The Father, the Son, and the Holy Spirit.

Just as Christ lifted the fruit,
From the branches of the bushes,
May He now lift from you
Every ailment, every envy, every jealousy,
From this day forward until the final day of your life.

<vocab>
**Evil Eye**: A superstitious belief in a curse cast by a malevolent glare.
**Hag** <note>original: "carlin"</note>: A term for an old woman, often associated with witchcraft in folklore.
**Luck/Charm** <note>original: "seoin"</note>: A Gaelic word relating to one's state of fortune or magical standing.
**Incantation**: A series of words said as a magic spell or charm.
</vocab>
