# KNOWLEDGE
<note>original: "EOLAS," a Gaelic term meaning knowledge, but in this context specifically referring to a healing charm or a secret medicinal prayer.</note>

<image-desc>An ornate, Celtic-style decorative initial 'P' featuring a zoomorphic beast head at the top terminal and intricate interlaced knotwork within the bowl of the letter. The descender of the letter is long and slender, ending in a stylized knot at the bottom.</image-desc>

> **P**eter and James and John,
> The three of sweetest virtues in glory,
> Rose to perform the incantation <note>original: "na h-eoir"</note>,
> Before the great door of the City <note>original: "na Cathrach," typically referring to the City of God or the Kingdom of Heaven</note>,
> At the right knee of God the Son.
>
> ->Against the sharp-eyed men,<-
> ->Against the heavy-eyed women,<-
> ->Against the slender, elfish sprites,<- <note>original: "siocharra seanga sith," referring to fairy folk or supernatural beings believed to cause harm</note>
> ->Against the swift, whistling arrows.<- <note>original: "saighde," referring to "elf-bolts" or "fairy arrows," which were believed to be the cause of sudden, unexplained pains or illnesses in livestock and people</note>
>
> ->Two people who brought you the eye's destruction,<-
> ->A man and a woman with venom and envy,<-
> ->Three I place as a guarantee against them:<-
> ->The Father, the Son, and the Holy Spirit.<-
>
> Twenty-four diseases dwell in the constitution of man and beast; may God scrap them, God sweep them, God search them out, from your blood, your flesh, and your sweet, fair bones, from this day and every day that follows, until the final day of your life arrives.

<vocab>
**Incantation** <note>original: "Eolas" or "Uibe"</note>: A ritual chant or prayer used for healing or protection in Highland folklore.
**The City** <note>original: "Cathrach"</note>: A common poetic term for the Kingdom of Heaven.
**The Eye** <note>original: "sul"</note>: Referring to the "Evil Eye," a look believed to be able to cause injury or bad luck to others.
**Surety** <note>original: "urra"</note>: A person who takes responsibility for another; here, the Trinity acts as a spiritual guard.
</vocab>

# INCANTATIONS

## CHARM

Peter and James and John,
The three of sweetest virtues in glory,
Who arose to perform the charm <note>original: "na h-eoir," refers to the specific ritual or prayer being recited</note>,
Before the great gate of the City <note>The City of God or the Kingdom of Heaven</note>,
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;At the right knee of God the Son.

Against the sharp-eyed men,
Against the prying-eyed women <note>These lines refer to the "Evil Eye," a look believed to cause harm through envy or malice</note>,
Against the slim, slender fairy-darts <note>original: "saighde," referring to "elf-shot" or flint arrowheads found in the soil, which were believed to be weapons of the fairy folk used to strike cattle or people</note>,
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;Against the swift arrows of the furies.

Two people cast the withered eye <note>A look intended to blight or destroy health</note> upon you,
A man and a woman with venom and envy,
Three I will set against them:
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;The Father, the Son, and the Holy Spirit.

Twenty-four diseases within the constitution of man and beast,
May God scrape them, may God search them, may God cleanse them,
From out of your blood, from out of your flesh, from out of your fair bones <note>original: "cneamhach," which can mean fragrant or beautiful, often used in Gaelic charms to describe the health and purity of the skeletal structure</note>,
From this day and every day to come, until your days on earth are done.

<vocab>
**Peter, James, John:** Three of Christ's apostles frequently invoked in Scottish Gaelic folklore for protection.
**Charm:** A spoken prayer or ritual intended to heal or protect.
**The City:** Heaven.
**God the Son:** Jesus Christ.
**Sharp-eyed men/Prying-eyed women:** Those capable of casting the "Evil Eye."
**Fairy-darts:** Supernatural missiles believed to cause sudden physical pain or illness.
**Furies:** In this context, harmful spiritual forces or sudden destructive winds.
**Withered eye:** The damaging effect of the Evil Eye.
**Venom and envy:** The spiritual "poison" behind a malicious look.
**Father, Son, and Holy Spirit:** The Holy Trinity.
**Twenty-four diseases:** A traditional number used in Gaelic charms to represent a complete set of ailments.
**Man and beast:** Humans and their livestock (cattle, sheep, etc.), both of which were vital to rural survival.
</vocab>

<summary>This page contains a protective Gaelic charm invoking the apostles Peter, James, and John to heal ailments and ward off the "Evil Eye" and fairy-related illnesses. It emphasizes the power of the Trinity to cleanse the body of twenty-four distinct diseases affecting both humans and animals.</summary>

<keywords>incantation, eolas, charm, Evil Eye, fairy-darts, Peter James and John, Gaelic folklore, healing prayer, Trinity</keywords>

### A CURSE
<note>original: "Mallachd." While titled a "curse," this is a counter-curse or exorcism intended to turn back the harm caused by others' malice.</note>

> From the City of Heaven <note>original: "Cathrach Neobh"</note>,
> A man and a woman,
> To perform the incantations <note>original: "nan oisnean," literally "corners," referring to the ritual practice of chanting charms at the four corners of a dwelling or field for complete protection</note>.

> Curse the keen-eyed men,

> That might be in the constitution <note>original: "aorabh," the physical or spiritual health</note> of man and beast.

### INCANTATION FOR THE EYE-BITE
<note>original: "Eolas a Bheim Shuil." An "Eolas" is a healing charm or "knowledge." "Beum-shuil" or "Eye-bite" is the traditional term for injury caused by the Evil Eye.</note>

The following fragment was copied from an old manuscript and sent to me by the

<margin>[the shore?]</margin>
> I will trample upon the eye,
> As a swan tramples on a bare house <note>A metaphor for overwhelming and crushing the power of the malicious glance</note>,
> I have the strength of the wind over it,
> I have the strength of the sun over it,
> I have the strength of the Son of the King of Heaven
> And of the Earth over it,
> A third of the harm upon the grey stones,

-> * &nbsp; &nbsp; &nbsp; * &nbsp; &nbsp; &nbsp; * &nbsp; &nbsp; &nbsp; * <-

> And a third upon the great sea,
> She herself <note>Likely referring to the Earth or the power of the incantation</note> is the best strength to bear it.

## SPELL OF THE EVIL EYE
<note>original: "Eolas a Bheim Shuil." The word "Eolas" literally means "knowledge" but refers here to a traditional healing formula or "charm." "Beum-shuil" is the "Eye-bite" or the injury caused by a malicious glance.</note>

This fragment was collected by the Reverend Angus Macdonald of Killearnan, Black Isle. The reciter’s name is recorded as "Anna Chaimbeul"—Ann Campbell.

> I trample upon the eye,
> As the swan tramples on the shore <gloss>[original: "bar house," likely a transcription of the Gaelic for a sandbar or strand]</gloss>,
> I have power of the wind over it,
> I have power of the sun over it,
> The power of the Son of the King of Heaven
> And of earth I have over it.
> A third of it <gloss>[portion]</gloss> be upon the gray stones,
> ->*&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;*&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;*&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;*<-
> And a third be upon the great sea;
> She herself <note>likely referring to the Earth or the Sea</note> is the most able instrument to bear it.

<page-num>69</page-num>
<header>INCANTATIONS</header>

## SPELL OF THE EYE
<note>original title: "Ob ri Shul." This charm is intended to protect a person or their property from the "evil eye"—a malevolent gaze believed to cause sickness or bad luck.</note>

The spell that great-white Mary sent
To Bridget <note>original: "Bride." Saint Bridget, often called "the Mary of the Gael," is a central figure in Celtic Christianity and folklore.</note> the lovely and fair,
Over sea, over land, over water, and against the withering glance,
Against the teeth of the wolf, against the testicle of the wolf <note>original: "uir-magadh madadh." This likely refers to a protective herb, such as the early purple orchid (*Orchis mascula*), known in some regions as "wolf’s testicle," or it may be a literal symbolic ward against predators.</note>.

Whoever laid the eye on you,
May it oppress themselves,
May it oppress their house,
May it oppress their flocks <note>original: "spreidh," meaning cattle or livestock.</note>.

Let me subdue the eye,
Let me turn away the eye,
By the three tongues of total fullness,
In the arteries of the heart,
In the vitals of the navel.

&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;From the bosom of the Father,
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;From the bosom of the Son,
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;From the bosom of the Holy Spirit.

<margin>[153]</margin>
### THE SPELL FOR WITHERING ENVY
<note>original: "Eolas a Chronachaidh." This charm is intended to counteract "chronachadh"—a specific type of harm caused by envy or the "evil eye," which was believed to cause cows to go dry or calves to sicken.</note>

<image-desc>A large, ornate initial letter 'U' in the Celtic style, formed from complex knotwork and interlaced ribbons. At the top left of the 'U', there is a zoomorphic head of a serpent or dragon with a wide-open mouth. The interlacing fills the body of the letter and extends into a decorative tail-like flourish at the bottom right.</image-desc>I pluck the seat of joy <note>original: "Uainidh mi a chathair aigh." The "cathair aigh" (literally "seat of joy") likely refers to a specific plant, such as common knapweed, used as a talisman in the ritual.</note>
Which Christ plucked with His one hand.

The High King <note>original: "Ard Righ." A common Gaelic title for God or Christ.</note> of the angels came
With His love and His protection over me.

Jesus Christ came in
With abundance, with richness, with cream <note>original: "blioc, le blac, le barr." These are archaic terms for various qualities of milk and dairy richness.</note>,
With female calves, with plenty of milk.

Against the small eye, against the large eye,
Protecting the property of Christ.

In the name of the Being of the Elements <note>original: "Ti nan dul." An ancient title for the Creator, encompassing all natural forces.</note>,
Keep Your grace with me,
O Crown of the King of the angels,
To put milk in the udder and in the yield,
With female calves, with healthy offspring.

May you have for the length of seven years
Without loss of calf, without loss of milk,
Without loss of wealth or a dear friend.

<page-num>72</page-num>
<header>UIBE</header> <note>This header likely refers to "Uibhist" (Uist), a region in the Outer Hebrides where these traditions were collected.</note>
<page-type>text</page-type>

## THE COUNTING OF THE STYE

The ritual to drive out <note>original: "exorcism"</note> a stye is known by several names: *Cunntas an t-Sleamhnain*—Counting of the Stye; *Eolas an t-Sleamhnain*—The Healing Knowledge <note>original: "Exorcism"</note> of the Stye; and *Eoir an t-Sleamhnain*—The Charm of the Stye.

When performing the charm, the practitioner <note>original: "exorcist"</note> holds a sharp-pointed instrument, preferably a nail, or the pin <note>original: "tongue"</note> of a brooch or buckle, between the thumb and forefinger of the right hand. With each question, the operator makes a feint with the instrument at the stye, coming dangerously <note>original: "perilously"</note> near the eye. The sensation caused by the thrusting motion is extremely painful to the sufferer and even to the observer.

The reciter promised <note>original: "assured"</note> the writer that a cure immediately follows the operation. It is possible that the thrusting motion acts upon the patient's nervous system.

Ordinarily, the healer omits mentioning the word ‘stye’ <note>original: "sleamhnan"</note> after the first two times, shortening the charm like this:

| | |
|---|---|
| ‘Why did the two come here | <note>original: ‘C’um an tainig a dha an seo’</note> |
| Without the three being here?’ | <note>original: ‘Gun a tri an seo?’</note> |

---

<margin>[Why do they come</margin> <note>original: "Com a thig"</note>
<image-desc>A large ornamental zoomorphic initial 'C' executed in Celtic knotwork style. The body of the letter is formed by complex interlace patterns, featuring a beast's head at the top right corner with a visible eye and pointed ear, and a tail or second head at the bottom right biting into the knotwork.</image-desc>
> **WHY** did the one stye come, <note>original: "C’UIM an tainig an aon sleamhnan"</note>
> Without the two styes being here?
> Why did the two styes come,
> Without the three styes being here?
> Why did the three styes come,
> Without the four styes being here?
> Why did the four styes come,
> Without the five styes being here?
> Why did the five styes come,
> Without the six styes being here?
> Why did the six styes come,
> Without the seven styes being here?
> Why did the seven styes come,
> Without the eight styes being here?
> Why did the eight styes come,
> Without the nine styes being here?
> Why did the nine come,
> Or even one at all here? <note>original: "No aon idir an seo?"</note>

<summary>This page details a traditional Gaelic folk cure for a stye, involving a rhythmic counting rhyme accompanied by a physical ritual using a sharp object to startle or stimulate the patient.</summary>
<keywords>Cunntas an t-Sleamhnain, stye cure, Gaelic folklore, folk medicine, charms, exorcism, ritual healing, Outer Hebrides</keywords>

# INCANTATIONS

## THE COUNTING OF THE STYE

After the incantation, the Lord’s Prayer <note>original Latin: "Pater"</note> is recited, and the following is repeated:

| | |
|---|---|
| Lord's Prayer one, <note>original Gaelic: "Paidir a h-aon"</note> | Lord's Prayer one, |
| Lord's Prayer two, <note>original Gaelic: "Paidir a dha"</note> | Lord's Prayer two, |
| Lord's Prayer three, <note>original Gaelic: "Paidir a tri"</note> | Lord's Prayer three, |
| Lord's Prayer four, <note>original Gaelic: "Paidir a ceithir"</note> | Lord's Prayer four, |
| Lord's Prayer five, <note>original Gaelic: "Paidir a coig"</note> | Lord's Prayer five, |
| Lord's Prayer six, <note>original Gaelic: "Paidir a sia"</note> | Lord's Prayer six, |
| Lord's Prayer seven, <note>original Gaelic: "Paidir a seachd"</note> | Lord's Prayer seven, |
| Lord's Prayer eight, <note>original Gaelic: "Paidir a h-ochd"</note> | Lord's Prayer eight, |
| Lord's Prayer nine, <note>original Gaelic: "Paidir a naodh"</note> | Lord's Prayer nine, |
| Lord's Prayer one <note>original Gaelic: "Paidir a h-aon"</note> | Lord's Prayer one |
| And eight, <note>original Gaelic: "’S a h-ochd"</note> | And eight, |
| Lord's Prayer of kindly Christ <note>original Gaelic: "Paidir Chriosda chaoimh"</note> | Lord's Prayer of kindly Christ |
| Be upon you tonight, <note>original Gaelic: "Ort an oidhche nochd"</note> | Be upon you tonight, |
| Lord's Prayer of the Trinity <note>original Gaelic: "Paidir Tri nan dul"; literally "the Three of the elements/creation," referring to the Holy Trinity</note> | Lord's Prayer of the Three of life |
| Upon your eye without harm. <note>original Gaelic: "Air a shuil gun lochd"</note> | Upon your eye without harm. |

This seems to indicate that the Lord's Prayer was originally repeated nine times.

<margin>[Why comes...]</margin> <note>original: "Com a thig"</note>
> WHY came the one stye,
> Without the two styes here?
> Why came the two styes,
> Without the three styes here?
> Why came the three styes,
> Without the four styes here?
> Why came the four styes,
> Without the five styes here?
> Why came the five styes,
> Without the six styes here?
> Why came the six styes,
> Without the seven styes here?
> Why came the seven styes,
> Without the eight styes here?
> Why came the eight styes,
> Without the nine styes here?
> Why came the nine,
> Or any at all here? <note>This repetitive counting down is a common feature in folk magic; by "counting" the ailment, the healer seeks to control it and eventually make it vanish into nothingness.</note>

<summary>This page details a rhythmic counting charm and the recitation of the Lord's Prayer used in Gaelic folk medicine to cure a stye. The ritual involves a sequence of prayers and a series of rhetorical questions designed to "count down" and eliminate the inflammation.</summary>

# INCANTATIONS

### THE FAIR WELCOME [155]
<note>original Gaelic title: "Am Fionn-Faoilidh"</note>

<image-desc>A large, intricate Celtic knotwork initial letter 'C' forming the start of the word 'Cuiream' (I put). The letter is composed of two stylized serpent or dragon figures whose bodies intertwine in complex loops and knots, with their heads meeting at the top right.</image-desc>

->I put the charm of the fair welcome around me,<-
->To drain away empty anger,<-
->To maintain my good reputation,<-
->As long as I remain alive on the earth.<-

->O Michael! grasp my hand,<-
->Deliver to me the friendship of God;<-
->If there be ill-will or lack of goodwill in my enemy,<-
->May Christ be between me and him,<-
->Oh, Christ be between me and him!<-

->If there be ill-will or lack of goodwill on my account,<-
->May Christ be between me and him,<-
->Oh, Christ be between me and him!<-

---

### INCANTATION FOR ENVY [156]
<note>original Gaelic title: "Eolas Tnu"</note>

These lines were obtained in Tiree from a woman known as 'Nic ’aldomhnuich,' the daughter of Maoldomhnuich (a name often translated as 'Ludovic'). This woman had known many such runes, but was forgetting them. 

The name Maoldomhnuich is one of many personal names originating in the ancient Celtic Church. While these names are now rare elsewhere, they are still common in the Western Isles. The meanings of these names are particularly interesting, as the prefix *Maol* refers to someone who is "tonsured" (having the monastic haircut), signifying they are a servant or devotee of a specific saint.

*   **Maoldomhnuich** means 'the tonsured servant of the Lord.' <note>original Gaelic: "Maoldomhnuich"</note>
*   **Maolciaran** means 'the tonsured servant of Saint Ciaran.' <note>original Gaelic: "Maolciaran"</note>
*   **Maolpadruig** means 'the tonsured servant of Saint Patrick.' <note>original Gaelic: "Maolpadruig"</note>
*   **Maolcalum** means 'the tonsured servant of Saint Columba' (the origin of the name Malcolm). <note>original Gaelic: "Maolcalum"</note>
*   **Maolmicheil** means 'the tonsured servant of Saint Michael.' <note>original Gaelic: "Maolmicheil"</note>
*   **Maolbride** means 'the tonsured servant of Saint Brigid.' <note>original Gaelic: "Maolbride"</note>
*   **Maolmoire** means 'the tonsured servant of the Virgin Mary.' <note>original Gaelic: "Maolmoire"</note>
*   **Maoliosa** means 'the tonsured servant of Jesus'—this is the name 'Malise' used by Sir Walter Scott. <note>original Gaelic: "Maoliosa"</note>

The incantation for envy or jealousy <note>term: "tnu" refers to a mixture of envy, jealousy, or a "malicious eye" cast by another</note> follows:

->Whoever has caused envy toward you,<-
->Whether a dark-haired man or a fair-haired woman,<-
->Three I send to them to restrain it—<-
->The Holy Spirit, the Father, and the Son.<-

<summary>This page features two Gaelic charms: one for graciousness and protection from enemies, and another to ward off envy. It also explains the religious origins of traditional Gaelic names beginning with "Maol," signifying a "tonsured servant" of a saint.</summary>
<keywords>incantations, Am Fionn-Faoilidh, Eolas Tnu, St. Michael, Gaelic names, tonsure, Maoldomhnuich, Tiree folk traditions, charms against envy</keywords>

# INCANTATIONS

## THE ‘FIONN-FAOILIDH’ <note>The Fair Welcome</note>

> I place the ‘fionn-faoilidh’ <note>original: *fionn-faoilidh*; a charm to ensure a fair welcome or pleasant reception</note> upon me,
> To drain away all anger,
> To preserve my good reputation,
> As long as I shall live on earth.
> 
> O Michael! grasp my hand,
> Grant to me the love of God,
> If there is ill-will or a bad wish in my enemy,
> May Christ be between me and him,
> ->Oh, Christ be between me and him!<-
> 
> If there is ill-will or a bad wish concerning me,
> May Christ be between me and it,
> ->Oh, Christ be between me and it!<-

---

## ENVY SPELL

...the Malise <note>original: "Malise," a name derived from *Maol-Iosa*, meaning 'the tonsured servant of Jesus'</note> of the Earls of Strathearn. A steep island near the east entrance to Macneilltown, Barra, is called ‘Maoldomhnuich’ <note>the tonsured servant of the Lord</note> after a hermit <note>original: "anchorite"</note> of that name who lived there and whose cell is still to be seen. The island is also called ‘Eilean nam fiadh’ <note>Isle of the deer</note>, because the ancient Macneills of Barra kept deer there.

There is hardly an island, however remote, or an ocean-surrounded rock, however steep, throughout the stormy Hebridean seas that does not show touching traces of the courage and devotion of these self-denying hermits. The writer often took pleasure in visiting these almost inaccessible rocks and tracing the remains of their cells.

> Whoever directed envy toward you,
> Whether a dark-haired man or a fair woman,
> I will send three to thwart it—
> The Holy Spirit, the Father, and the Son.

<summary>This page concludes an explanation of Gaelic names derived from the early Celtic Church and provides charms for social protection and warding off envy. It also highlights the historical presence of Christian hermits (anchorites) throughout the Hebrides.</summary>

## THE RED-STALK

I will pluck the little red-stalk of security <note>original: *surety*</note>,
The fiber <note>original: *lint*</note> that the lovely Bride <note>St. Bridget, often called the "Mary of the Gaels" and the patroness of livestock</note> drew through her palm,
For the blessing <note>original: *success*</note> of health, for the blessing of friendship,
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;For the blessing of joyfulness,
For victory over <note>original: *overcoming of*</note> an evil mind, for victory over the evil eye,
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;For victory over witchcraft <note>original: *bewitchment*</note>,
For victory over evil deeds, for victory over evil behavior,
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;For victory over a curse <note>original: *malediction*</note>,
For victory over bad news, for victory over evil words,
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;For the blessing of joy—
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;For the blessing of joy.

## THE TREE-ENTWINING IVY

I will pluck the tree-entwining ivy,
As Mary plucked it with her own hand,
As the King of Life <note>original: *Righ nan dul*; a Gaelic title for God as King of the elements or the universe</note> has ordained,
To bring milk to the udder and the teat <note>original: *gland*</note>,
With beautiful, speckled female calves,
As was spoken in the prophecy,
Upon this homestead <note>original: *foundation*</note> for a year and a day,
Through the grace <note>original: *bosom*</note> of the God of Life and all the heavenly powers.

<meta>This page continues the exploration of sacred plants and their traditional uses in Gaelic folklore. While the previous page focused on charms for security and milk production using the "red-stalk" and ivy, this page discusses the figwort, specifically its naming, medicinal properties, and its association with ancient deities and the Beaton family of healers.</meta>

<page-num>78</page-num>
<header>THE KNOWLEDGE OF THE FIGWORT</header>
<meta>[159]</meta>

## THE KNOWLEDGE OF THE FIGWORT
<note>original: Eolas an Torranain</note>

The figwort <note>the genus *Scrophularia*</note> is known by many names: 'black mallet,' 'brown mallet,' 'noise,' 'forum,' 'crumbs,' 'refuse,' 'scrofulous,' 'plant of the clusters,' 'plant of the bells,' 'clustered,' 'plant of the thunderer,' 'thundering,' and 'thunderer.' <note>original Gaelic names: *farach dubh, farach donn, farum, forum, fothlus, fotlus, lus nan cnapan, lus nan clugan, clugan, cluganach, lus an torranain, torranach, and torranan*</note>. The names are descriptive: 'black mallet' and 'brown mallet' refer to its appearance; 'noise' and 'forum' are likely variations of the word for mallet. 'Crumbs,' 'refuse,' and 'scrofulous' refer to its use in treating skin conditions. 'Plant of the clusters' describes its growth habit, and 'plant of the thunderer' suggests a deeper history. It is probable that the names 'tarrann,' 'torrann,' 'torranan,' and 'tarranan' are variants of Taranis, the name of the thunder god of the ancient Gauls.

On the mainland, the figwort is known for its medicinal properties, while in the islands it is known for its magical powers. On the mainland, the leaf of the plant is applied to cuts and bruises, and the tuberous root is used for sores and tumors. In the islands, the plant was placed on the cow’s leg-tether, under the milk bowl <note>original: *boyne*</note>, and over the stable door to ensure the cows produced plenty of milk.

After reciting the incantation of the 'thunderer,' the speaker said: "The 'thunderer' is a blessed plant. It grows in sight of the sea. Its root is a cluster of four bulbs, like the four teats of a cow. The stalk of the plant is as long as a person's arm, and the bloom is as large as a woman's breast, and as pure white as the driven snow on the hill. It is full of the milk of grace and goodness, and possessed of the gift of peace and power; it fills with the rising tide and ebbs with the falling tide. It is therefore proper to harvest <note>original: *cull*</note> the plant with the flow and not with the ebb of the restless sea. If I had the 'thunderer,' it would ensure that my cow gave abundant milk all year. As poor as I am, I would rather have the blessed 'thunderer' than a British pound. I went away to John, the son of Fearachar—who knows every plant that grows from the ground—to see if he would get me the 'thunderer' of power. But John's wife said 'No,' and told me I was only a silly woman <note>original: *oinig*</note>. The hussy! <note>original: *jade*</note>"

John Beaton—known as John, son of Fearachar, son of John, son of 'Neil the Doctor' <note>original: *Niall Dotair*</note>—was a shepherd by trade but a botanist by instinct. He spoke only Gaelic and was illiterate, but he likely knew more about plants, their habitats, and their characteristics than any other man in Scotland. He lived in close communion with nature and loved plants as he loved his children—with a warm, lasting love that no poverty could cool and no age could dim. A Gaelic proverb says: "That was natural for him" <note>original: *Bu dual da sin*—meaning it was in his blood</note>; and: "Heredity will split the rock" <note>original: *Sgoiltidh an dualchas a chreag*</note>; and again: "Heredity will go against the rocks" <note>original: *Theid dualchas an aghaidh nan creag*</note>. John Beaton was a striking proof of these sayings, being descended from a long line of botanists and doctors who left their mark on the minds and the language of their fellow countrymen. He was descended from the famous Beatons of Skye, who were themselves descended from...

<summary>This page discusses the names and folklore of the figwort plant, connecting it to the Gaulish god Taranis and detailing its medicinal and magical uses. It also introduces John Beaton, a shepherd with an extraordinary hereditary knowledge of botany.</summary>
<keywords>figwort, Taranis, John Beaton, Gaelic folklore, ethnobotany, Beatons of Skye, traditional medicine, cattle charms</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the discussion of the figwort (torranan) by tracing the lineage of the Beaton family, the famous hereditary physicians of the Scottish Highlands and Islands who preserved much of this traditional botanical and medical knowledge.</meta>

<page-num>79</page-num>
<header>INCANTATIONS</header>
<page-type>text</page-type>

## THE CHARM OF THE FIGWORT

...the Beatons of Islay. They, in turn, were descended from the Beatons of Mull, who are said to have come down from Beatan, the medical missionary of the Columban Church of Iona. These Beatons produced many eminent men, among them James Beaton, the Archbishop of Glasgow, and his even more famous nephew David Beaton, the Cardinal Archbishop of St. Andrews. Through the Barons Livingstone of Bachuill in Lismore, this lineage also includes David Livingstone, the celebrated physician, missionary, traveler, and explorer. Mary Beaton, mentioned in the famous ballad of the Queen’s *Four Maries*, was also a member of this family:

> "Last night there were four Maries,
> This night there shall be but three:
> There was Mary Beaton and Mary Seton
> And Mary Carmichael and me."

The people of Mull say that this Mary Beaton was from the Mull branch of the family, but the distinguished scholar, the late Hector Maclean, and other men from Islay, claimed she belonged to the Islay Beatons. The Beatons were hereditary <term>physicians</term> <note>original Gaelic: *leighhean*</note> to the Lords of the Isles and to other great island and mainland chiefs. They were also physicians to the Kings of Scotland, whom they visited periodically. Payments for some of these royal visits are recorded in the official government accounts <note>original: Exchequer Rolls</note>.

The Beatons left behind many <term>manuscripts</term> <note>original: *mss.*</note> regarding medicine and medicinal plants. Some of these are kept in the Advocates’ Library, some are in private collections, and many are known to have been lost. Some of the most beautiful carved stones in Iona, Mull, Islay, and elsewhere mark the tombs of the Beatons.

Several of the Beatons from Mull and Islay traveled to Paris and other European cities to complete their medical and theological studies. Some remained abroad and rose to high positions. The name is still found in France in its French form, Bethune. One of the Beatons, upon returning to Scotland, kept that version of the name and settled in Fife. A descendant of his later settled in Skye as a <term>physician</term> <note>original: leech</note> to the Macleod of Macleod, founding the Skye branch of the family. One member of this family was known as <term>Farquhar the Physician</term> <note>original Gaelic: *Fearachar Leigh* or *Fearachar Lighiche*</note>. He held the small estate of Husabost, near the mouth of Dunvegan Loch, as payment for his medical services. He owned a medical <term>manuscript</term> <note>original: *ms.*</note> valued at sixty milk cows; he was so protective of this document that when he traveled to Dunvegan by boat, he sent a trusted manservant on horseback by land with the manuscript to ensure its safety. John Beaton, the shepherd of Uist, was a descendant of this "Farquhar the Physician."

John Beaton was too old and suffered too much from rheumatism to leave his home, but he described the <term>figwort</term> <note>original Gaelic: *torranan*</note>—its flower, leaf, stalk, and root, and where it grows on the mountain of Ben More—to his son and the author with marvelous detail and accuracy, even though he had not been...

<summary>This page details the history of the Beaton family, a renowned lineage of hereditary physicians in the Scottish Isles who were keepers of medical manuscripts and traditional botanical knowledge. It links these historical figures to the modern-day informants who provided the descriptions of the figwort plant.</summary>
<keywords>Beatons, Islay, Mull, hereditary physicians, Mary Beaton, Four Maries, Farquhar the Physician, medical manuscripts, figwort, Gaelic medicine, David Livingstone</keywords>

--- Page 100 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of the figwort plant (torranan) and the Beaton family, transitioning from the history of these hereditary physicians to a specific account of the plant's location on Benmore and the legend of Saint Torranan's arrival in the Hebrides.</meta>

<page-num>80</page-num>
<header>UIST</header>

John Beaton had not been to Benmore or seen the <term>‘torranan’</term> <note>original: *torranan*; the figwort plant, specifically *Scrophularia nodosa*</note> for many years. He said there were only two such plants in Uist, located near each other on Benmore overlooking the sea. He explained the various medicinal uses of the plant but smiled at its alleged magical powers.

This was in 1877. John Beaton died in 1881 at the age of 92; he was both a natural scientist and a true gentleman. In 1896, his son Fearachar sent me the two plants from Benmore in South Uist. I gave one of them to Professor Bayley Balfour at the University of Edinburgh, who kindly identified the plant for me.

The following legend is common in Uist: The Pope sent Torranan <note>likely Saint Ternan, a 5th-century Christian missionary</note> to teach the people of Ireland the way of salvation. But the Irish people would not receive Torranan; they beat and mistreated him in various ways. Torranan prayed to God to deliver him from the Irish and shook the dust of Ireland off his feet. He went to his <term>coracle</term> <note>a small, lightweight boat made of a waterproof layer over a wooden frame</note> and turned it clockwise <note>original: "sun-wise"; in Gaelic tradition, moving clockwise (deiseil) was considered auspicious and holy</note> in the name of God, in the name of Christ, and in the name of the Spirit. He prayed to the <term>‘Teora Naomh,’</term> <note>original Gaelic: *Teora Naomh*; the Holy Three or the Trinity</note> to send him whenever and wherever they wished and had work for him to do—as long as it was not back to Ireland. The man was tossed about here and there on the wild waves in his fragile boat; no one knows for how long or how far. But an Eye watched over his prow, and a Hand was on his helm. The tide, the wind, and the waves combined to bring him into the little creek of Cailigeo in Benbecula.

The island of Benbecula is situated between the islands of South Uist and North Uist. Its center runs at a right angle to those islands—one end faces the Minch <note>the strait between the Outer Hebrides and the Scottish mainland</note> and the other faces the Atlantic. It can be crossed on foot at low tide from both sides, which is why it has the Gaelic name <term>‘Beinn-nam-faoghla’</term> <note>original Gaelic: *Beinn-nam-faoghla*; meaning "mountain of the fords"</note>. The hill mentioned in the name is near the center of the island, almost in a direct line between the two fords. It is called <term>‘Ruaidhbhal’</term> or <term>‘Ruaival’</term>—meaning Red Hill—from the Gaelic <term>‘ruadh’</term> (red) and the Old Norse <term>‘fell’</term> (a hill). Ruaival is the only hill in Benbecula. It is cone-shaped, with a flat and level top, standing 409 feet high. The sloping sides are covered with heather, while the flat summit is green and grassy. The summit offers an extraordinary view of fords and channels, islands, peninsulas and mainlands, seas and lakes, and of moors and <term>machairs</term> <note>low-lying, fertile coastal plains unique to the northwest of Scotland and Ireland</note>. These are broken up and dotted with a marvelous number of shallow pools and lakes, scattered beyond count. Probably the world does not contain anything more chaotic than the distribution of land and water in and around Benbecula.

As Torranan was ascending the round red hill of Ruaival to survey his surroundings and find his bearings, his throat was parched with thirst, for he had not had any water to drink since leaving Ireland. Torranan prayed to God for water to quench his thirst, and suddenly, the red rock before him split apart, and a clear stream of cold water flowed from the crack. Thus, Torranan experienced the truth of Goethe’s words:

> ‘At his appointed time revolving,
> The sun these shades of night dispels,
> The rock, its rugged breast dissolving,
> Gives up to earth its hidden wells.’

<summary>This page recounts the botanical identification of the 'torranan' (figwort) plant in the late 19th century and relates the legend of Saint Torranan, who fled Ireland to settle in Benbecula after a miraculous sea journey.</summary>
<keywords>Torranan, Saint Ternan, Ruaival, Benbecula, Uist, Beinn-nam-faoghla, figwort, Teora Naomh, machairs, John Beaton, Professor Bayley Balfour, folklore, Hebrides geography</keywords>

--- Page 101 ---
<meta>This page continues the account of the missionary Torranan (St. Ternan) and his establishment of a religious site in the Outer Hebrides.</meta>

<page-num>81</page-num>
<header>INCANTATIONS</header>

## INCANTATIONS

The water was beautiful to see and pleasing to taste, and Torranan drank his "seven satiations" <note>Scottish Gaelic: *seachd sath*; a traditional expression for drinking until completely satisfied</note>, and he blessed the rill <note>a small stream</note> flowing from the cleft rock and called it "Gamhnach"—meaning a farrow cow <note>a cow that is not giving milk; the name implies the stream is as reliable as a cow that doesn't need to be dried off for calving</note>. "And Torranan beseeched the great God of the elements that the 'Gamhnach' might never go dry" <note>original: *Agus ghuidh Torranan air Dia mor nan dul nach d’reathadh a Ghamhnach gu brath an diosg*</note>. Ever since then, all pilgrims who go to the "Gamhnach" and drink from the stream give a choice green leaf to the "farrow cow" in memory of the refreshing drink it provided to the holy man who came to teach the people of "Innis Cat"—the Isle of the Caty <note>an old name for the area inhabited by the Catte tribe</note>—the way of salvation.

The man rejected by the people of Ireland became the accepted missionary of the people of Uist. He wished to build his prayer-house on "Cnoc Feannaig," the knoll of the hooded crow, within sight and hearing of the wild waves of Cailigeo where he had been driven ashore from his perilous voyage. Accordingly, he began to gather stones to build himself a prayer-house on the knoll. But the stones that Torranan collected on the knoll during the day, the spirits transferred by night to the island in the adjoining lake. After a time, Torranan gave up the unequal contest, saying that it was not right for him to set his will against the will of God as revealed by His angels. Then Torranan built his prayer-house on the little island, within hearing but not within seeing of the green seas and white waves of Cailigeo. And when the house was finished, Torranan dedicated the labor of his hands and the subject of his prayers to God and to Columba <note>St. Columba, the famous Irish abbot and missionary who spread Christianity in Scotland</note>.

The lake containing the islet on which the seafarer built his oratory <note>a small chapel for private prayer</note> is now lowered, and what was formerly an island is now a peninsula jutting into the lake. The oratory said to have been built by Torranan is a ruin. The ruin shows an extension of the original building. This extension is said to have been made by Amie, daughter of Ruairi mac Allan, High Chief of Lorn, and wife of John of Islay, Lord of the Isles. Shell lime is used in the extension ascribed to the Lady Amie, but not in the original structure ascribed to Torranan. Captain Thomas, Royal Navy, to whom the antiquities and archaeology of the Outer Hebrides owe much, said that the part of the church ascribed to Torranan might well belong to the Columban period <note>roughly the 6th century AD</note>. The Columban churches are believed to have been usually constructed of wattles <note>interwoven sticks and twigs plastered with mud</note>. But there were no wattles nor wood of any kind in Uist as late as Columba’s time. Consequently, in this and similar situations, the Columban brethren and followers had to depart from their usual practice and build of stone.

The lake containing the peninsula on which Torranan built his prayer-house, dedicated to Columba, is called "Loch Chaluim-chille"—Columba’s Loch. It only covers an area of a few acres and is of no great depth. Cairns <note>mounds of stones built as a memorial or landmark</note> and crosses once dotted the many knolls and hillocks surrounding the lake. But no trace of cairn or cross now remains. These pious offerings of a grateful people and of a bygone age to the memory of the saint have been secularized and used for making roads and building culverts <note>tunnels for water under a road</note>.

A religious house was afterwards built on Cnoc Feannaig, where Torranan had originally wished to build his prayer-house. It is now, and has been for centuries, a dwelling-house, and is probably the oldest inhabited house in Scotland.

<summary>This page describes the legendary founding of a prayer-house by the missionary Torranan on Uist, including the miraculous movement of stones to a lake island and the site's later architectural history. It notes the transition of the site from a sacred space to a secular one, with ancient monuments repurposed for modern infrastructure.</summary>

<keywords>Torranan, St. Columba, Uist, Loch Chaluim-chille, Lady Amie, archaeology, Hebrides, Cnoc Feannaig, ecclesiastical architecture, folklore</keywords>

--- Page 102 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of Saint Tarran (Torranan) and his associations with specific locations in the Hebrides and eastern Scotland, followed by a ritual incantation for livestock.</meta>

<page-num>82</page-num>
<header>UIST</header>

Torranan is represented in the West on the island of "Taransay"—Tarran’s island. On this small, rocky, glaciated island in the Atlantic, there were two small churches, of which nothing now remains but the foundations, each with a small burial ground attached. The churches are beautifully situated on the seashore near one another, looking across to the ice-rounded mountains of Harris and Uist, while in the far-away blue distance, the jagged, scorched-looking hills of Skye are visible.

One of these simple churches and its burial ground was dedicated to Saint Tarran and called *Teampull Tharrain*—the Temple of Tarran, and *Cladh Tharrain*—the burial place of Tarran. The other church and burial ground were dedicated to Saint Ce (or Keith) and were called *Teampull Che*—the Temple of Ce, and *Cladh Che*—the burial place of Ce. The temple and burial ground of Tarran were exclusively for the use of women, while the temple and burial ground of Ce were exclusively for the use of men. This rule could not be violated without consequences. If the body of a man were buried in St. Tarran’s, or the body of a woman in St. Ce’s, the guardian spirits of the temples and burial grounds thrust out the intruding corpse during the night; it would be found in the morning lying stiff and cold above ground. In North Uist, there is a tall obelisk called *Clach Che*—the stone of Ce. Saint Ce is represented in the East by *Beinn Che*—Benachie (the hill of Ce), *Innis Che*—Inchkeith (the island of Ce), and *Dail Che*—Dalkeith (the plain of Ce).

Palladius is the name usually assigned to the missionary sent by the Pope to the Irish and rejected by them. Skene <note>William Forbes Skene (1809–1892), a famous Scottish historian and antiquary.</note> thinks that Ternan was a disciple of Palladius, and that the two are often confused.

<image-desc>A large, ornate initial 'U' decorated with intricate Celtic knotwork and zoomorphic elements. The letter forms a circular base with an upward-curving arm. Within the circular part, there are stylized bird-like creatures intertwined with the knotwork. The upper part of the 'U' also features knotwork and a stylized animal head.</image-desc>

I will cull the figwort <note>original: *torranan*; likely the Water Figwort, used in folk charms for cattle</note>,
With the fruit of sea and land,
It is the plant of joy and happiness,
The plant of the creamy milk.

As the King of kings ordained,
To put substance in breast and nipple,
And as the Being of the elements <note>original: *Ti nan dul*; a Gaelic title for God</note> ordained,
To put sap in udder and vein,
With milk, with cream, with substance,
With froth, with whisked curd, and with cheese,
With female calves, dappled,
Without any male calves among them,
With offspring, with prosperity, with fruitfulness,
With love, with kindness, with happiness.

<summary>This page describes the gender-segregated ancient chapels on Taransay dedicated to Saints Tarran and Ce, and provides a Gaelic blessing for the abundance of milk and female livestock.</summary>
<keywords>Saint Tarran, Saint Ce, Taransay, Teampull Tharrain, Inchkeith, Dalkeith, Palladius, Skene, Torranan, cattle blessing, Gaelic folklore, Hebrides</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the discussion of Celtic saints and the folklore surrounding specific plants and locations, transitioning into a rhythmic incantation or charm.</meta>

<page-num>83</page-num>
<header>INCANTATIONS</header>

# INCANTATIONS

Ternan was buried at Liconium, or My Toren of Tulach, Fort-chirn, in Ne Felmada, and Druim Cliat in Cairbre. Skene <note>William Forbes Skene (1809–1892), a noted Scottish historian and antiquary.</note> thinks that Liconium was the old name of Banchory-Ternan on the river Dee in Aberdeenshire.

The feast of St. Ternan is the 12th of June. Like St. Brendan of Clonfert, St. Ternan was a seafarer who visited many countries. He is spoken of as *“Torranan buan bannach darler lethan longach”*—"Torranan the lasting and deedful, over the wide, ship-filled sea." <note>original: Gaelic phrase describing the saint's seafaring nature.</note> Many popular stories and distinctive names are attached to him.

The plant named after him <note>The "torranan," often identified as the figwort or water betony.</note> is popularly supposed to grow only near the sea which Torranan loved. The small rill <note>term: a small stream or brook</note> from which Torranan obtained a drink is named *“Gamhnach,”* meaning a farrow cow—a cow that does not carry a calf, but which gives milk of good quality that is continuous but small in quantity. At present, a blade of any grass or the leaf of any plant is given to the *“Gamhnach”* as an offering. Probably it was once permissible for pilgrims who came to drink the water and to worship the *“Gamhnach”* to offer only the leaf of the *“torranan”* plant to the rill.

Another curious thing is that two streams into which the *“Gamhnach”* runs are called *“na Deathachan,”* the Dees, and that two lakes into which these streams flow are called *“Loch nan Deathachan fo dheas,”* the Loch of the Dees to the south, and *“Loch nan Deathachan fo thuath,”* the Loch of the Dees to the north. *“Dee”* and *“Deathachan”* are plurals of *“dia,”* meaning god. This root seems to occur also in Dundee, which is in Gaelic *“Dun-dia”*—hill of god, and *“Dun-diaigh”*—hill of gods, while the river on which it stands is called *“Tàth”* or *“Tàdh.”* Were these rivers once worshipped as gods?

St. Ternan forms a connecting link between the Dees of Benbecula <note>An island in the Outer Hebrides.</note> and the Dee of Aberdeen.

I WILL pluck the figwort,
With the fruitage of sea and land,
The plant of joy and gladness,
The plant of rich milk.

As the King of kings ordained,
To put milk in breast and gland,
As the Being of life ordained,
To place substance in udder and kidney,
With milk, with creaminess, with buttermilk,
With produce, with whisked whey, with milk-products,
With speckled female calves,
Without male calves,
With progeny, with joy, with fruitage,
With love, with charity, with bounty.

## THE FIGWORT
<note>original: AN TORRANAN. The Torranan is identified as the Water Figwort or Knotted Figwort, a plant believed to have medicinal and magical properties, especially for cattle.</note>

With its fruit as from the earth,

<header>HYMNAL</header>
<page-num>161</page-num>

### THE INCANTATION OF THE FIGWORT
<note>original: Eolas an Torranain. The "Torranan" is identified as the Figwort (Scrophularia nodosa), a plant believed to have great medicinal and magical properties, particularly for cattle.</note>

<image-desc>Ornate initial 'I' with intricate Celtic knotwork and a stylized animal head, possibly a dragon or serpent, forming part of the initial.</image-desc>

I WILL PLUCK the figwort,
With a thousand blessings, with a thousand virtues;
May St. Bridget be its sustenance for me,
May the gentle Mary be its fruitfulness for me,
Great Mary, the Mother who aids the people.

The nine joys have come,
With the nine waves of the sea,
To the plucking of the figwort,
With a thousand blessings, with a thousand virtues—
With a thousand blessings, with a thousand virtues.

The hand of Christ be with me,
The cause of Christ be before me,
The shadow of Christ be over me,
<margin>for the purpose of</margin>
My noble plant is for the purpose of being plucked—
My noble plant is for the purpose of being plucked.

In the name of the Father of Wisdom,
In the name of Christ of the Passion,
In the name of the Spirit of Grace,
In the dialogue of my death,
That he will not leave me until the Day of Judgment—
In the dialogue of my death,
That he will not leave me until the Day of Judgment.

<note>original: Luan. Literally "Monday," but used idiomatically in Gaelic to refer to the Day of Judgment (Di-Luain an t-Sleibhe).</note>

<summary>This page contains a ritual incantation used while gathering the figwort plant, invoking St. Bridget, the Virgin Mary, and the Trinity to bless the plant's medicinal virtues and protect the soul.</summary>

### THE CHARM OF THE FIGWORT
<note>The figwort (Scrophularia) was historically used in folk medicine to treat skin ailments and was believed to possess protective spiritual properties.</note>

I will gather the figwort,
Of a thousand blessings, of a thousand virtues,
The calm Bride <note>original: "Bride"; referring to St. Bridget, often associated with healing and the protection of women</note> endowing it to me,
The fair Mary enriching it for me,
The great Mary, the helping Mother of the people.

The nine joys came,
With the nine waves,
To gather the figwort,
Of a thousand blessings, of a thousand virtues—
Of a thousand blessings, of a thousand virtues.

The arm of Christ is around me,
The face of Christ is before me,
The shadow of Christ is over me,
While my noble plant is being gathered—
While my noble plant is being gathered.

In the name of the Father of wisdom,
In the name of the Christ of Easter <note>original: "Pasch"</note>,
In the name of the Spirit of grace,
Who, in the struggles of my death,
Will not leave me until Judgment Day <note>original: "Doom"</note>—
Who, in the struggles of my death,
Will not leave me until Judgment Day.

<header>THE EARNAID</header>

## THE PEACEFUL EARNAID
<note>original: An Earnaid Shith. The "Earnaid" is likely the Figwort or a similar medicinal herb used in folk charms.</note>

<margin>162</margin>
<image-desc>A large, ornate initial capital 'U' in the style of Celtic knotwork, featuring an intertwined serpentine or dragon-like creature with an open mouth and eye at the top.</image-desc> I will pluck the *earnaid*,
With a prayer from the bank,
To put an end to every trouble,
As long as it is an *earnaid*.

Peaceful *earnaid*, peaceful *earnaid*,
Happy is the person who possesses it;
There is nothing under the circuit of the sun
That it does not conquer with smooth success.

I will pluck the honored branch
That great Mary plucked, Mother of the people's help,
To cast off me every tale of scandal, gossip, and slander,
Lack of being, lack of mercy, lack of victory,
Falsehood, malice, deceit, and grief,
Until I go to the cold flagstone under the earth.

### THE YARROW <note>original: Earr Thalamhainn; literally "the tail of the earth." Yarrow was highly esteemed in Highland folklore for its medicinal and magical properties.</note> [163]

<page-num>96</page-num>
<header>ST. COLUMBA’S ARMPIT PACKAGE</header>

### ST. COLUMBA’S ARMPIT PACKAGE [165]

Saint John's wort is known by various names, all significant of the position of the plant in the minds of the people: *achlasan Chaluim-chille*, the armpit package of Columba; *caod Chaluim-chille*, the hail of Columba; *seun Chaluim-chille*, the charm of Columba; *seud Chaluim-chille*, the jewel of Columba; *allus Chaluim-chille*, the glory of Columba; *alla Mhoire*, the noble plant of Mary; and *alla-bhi* or *alla-bhuidhe*, the noble yellow plant. Possibly these are pre-Christian terms to which the endearing names of Mary and Columba were later added.

Saint John's wort is one of the few plants still cherished by the people to ward off second-sight <note>The supernatural ability to see future events or distant happenings</note>, enchantment, witchcraft, the evil eye, and death. It is also used to ensure peace and plenty in the house, increase and prosperity in the livestock pen, and growth and fruition in the field. The plant is secretly secured in the bodices of the women and in the vests of the men, under the left armpit. Saint John's wort, however, is effective only when the plant is found by accident. When this occurs, the joy of the finder is great and is gratefully expressed:

<image-desc>An ornate initial capital 'I' with intricate Celtic knotwork forming the body of the letter, terminating in a stylized animal head at the top left and a decorative tail at the bottom right.</image-desc>

I will pluck my armpit package, <note>original: *Buainidh mise m' achlasan*</note>
As a prayer to my King,
To check the hate of men of blood,
To check the frenzy of the foolish women. <margin>foolish/reckless</margin>

I will pluck my armpit package,
As a prayer to my King,
That mine may be the power of the armpit package
Over every person I see.

I will pluck my armpit package,
As a prayer to the Three, <note>The Holy Trinity</note>
Under the protection of the Trio of grace,
And Mary, Mother of Jesus.

<summary>This page describes the folklore surrounding Saint John's wort in the Scottish Highlands, detailing its various names associated with St. Columba and its use as a protective charm against supernatural harm.</summary>
<keywords>Saint John's wort, St. Columba, achlasan Chaluim-chille, folklore, protective charms, second-sight, Scottish Highlands, herbalism</keywords>

### INCANTATIONS

## SAINT JOHN'S WORT

<note>original: "Achlasan Chaluim-chille"</note>
    'The little armpit-package of Saint Columba,
    Without searching, without seeking!
    By the will of God and Christ,
    This year I shall not die.' <gloss>I shall not get [death]</gloss>
        Saint John's wort, Saint John's wort,
        Without search, without seeking!
        By the will of God and Christ Jesus,
        This year I shall not die.

It is specially prized when found in the fold of the flocks, as it foretells peace and prosperity to the herds throughout the year. The person who discovers it says:—

<note>original: "Alla bhi, alla bhi"</note>
    'Saint John's wort, Saint John's wort,
    Happy is the person who possesses it;
    The one who finds it in the fold of the young livestock,
    Shall never be without cattle.' <term>kine: an archaic word for cows or cattle</term>
        Saint John's wort, Saint John's wort,
        Happy those who have you,
        Whoever gets you in the herd's fold,
        Shall never be without cattle.

There is a tradition among the people that Saint Columba <note>The 6th-century Irish abbot who evangelized Scotland</note> carried the plant on his person because of his love and admiration for Saint John the Baptist—the one who went about preaching Christ and baptizing the converted, clothed in a garment of camel's hair and fed upon locusts and wild honey.

    I will gather my little plant,
    As a prayer to my King,
    To quiet the wrath of violent men,
    To check the deceits of seductive women.

    I will gather my little plant,
    As a prayer to my King,
    That mine may be its power
    Over all I see.

    I will gather my little plant,
    As a prayer to the Three <note>The Holy Trinity</note>,
    Beneath the shade of the Trinity of grace,
    And of Mary the Mother of Jesus.

<page-num>104</page-num>
<header>INCANTATIONS</header>
<page-num>169</page-num>

### THE ASPEN

The people of Uist say "that the hateful aspen is banned <note>original: *chroiseadh*; literally "crossed" or "excommunicated"</note> three times." The aspen is banned the first time because it haughtily held up its head while all the other trees of the forest bowed their heads lowly down as the King of all created things was being led to Calvary. And the aspen is banned the second time because it was chosen by the enemies of Christ for the cross upon which to crucify the Savior of mankind. And the aspen is banned the third time because—[here the reciter's memory failed him]. Hence:

<image-desc>A stylized, symmetrical image of a two-headed bird-like creature, possibly a double-headed eagle or a similar mythical bird, with scaly bodies and prominent eyes, standing on ornate feet.</image-desc>

A CURSE upon you, O aspen tree,
Upon you was hanged the King of the mountains,
Where the nail was struck without a blade,
And that driving of the crucifixion was very tight—
That driving of the crucifixion was very tight.

A curse upon you, O hard aspen,
Upon you was hanged the King of virtues,
The Sacrifice of Truth, the Lamb without stain,
And his blood in a torrent pouring down—
His blood in a torrent pouring down.

A curse upon you, O withered aspen,
Upon you was hanged the King of kings,
And cursed is every eye that sees,
Unless it curses you, O withered aspen—
Unless it curses you, O withered aspen!

<summary>This page records a Gaelic folk legend and poem from Uist explaining why the aspen tree is considered cursed, linking its "shaking" leaves to its pride and its role in the Crucifixion.</summary>
<keywords>aspen tree, Uist folklore, Crucifixion legends, Gaelic charms, religious poetry, Calvary, nature myths</keywords>

--- Page 125 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of folklore and incantations related to specific trees, focusing here on the superstitions surrounding the aspen.</meta>

<page-num>105</page-num>
<header>INCANTATIONS</header>

## THE ASPEN

The ever-tremulous, ever-quivering, ever-shaking motion of the guilty, hateful aspen occurs even in the stillest air. Clods of earth, stones, and other missiles, as well as curses, are hurled at the aspen by the people. The reciter—a man of much natural intelligence—said that he always took off his bonnet and cursed the hateful aspen in all sincerity wherever he saw it. No crofter <note>A tenant farmer who works a small plot of land (a croft).</note> in Uist <note>An island in the Outer Hebrides of Scotland.</note> would use aspen for his plough, his harrows, or for his farming implements of any kind. Nor would a fisherman use aspen for his boat, his baskets, or for any fishing gear whatsoever.

A curse <note>original: "Malison"</note> be upon you, O aspen tree!
Upon you was crucified the King of the mountains,
Into whom were driven the nails without a clenched end,
And that driving crucifixion was exceedingly painful—
That driving crucifixion was exceedingly painful.

A curse be upon you, O hard aspen!
Upon you was crucified the King of glory,
Sacrifice of Truth, Lamb without blemish,
His blood pouring down in streams—
His blood pouring down in streams.

A curse be upon you, O cursed aspen!
Upon you was crucified the King of kings,
And a curse be upon the eye that sees you,
If it does not curse <note>original: "maledict"</note> you, you cursed aspen—
If it does not curse you, you cursed aspen!

<summary>This page records the intense folk-prejudice against the aspen tree in the Scottish Highlands, detailing how its wood is shunned in labor and cursed in verse due to the legend that it provided the wood for the Cross of Christ.</summary>
<keywords>aspen tree, folklore, Uist, crofters, malison, crucifixion, religious superstition, Scottish Highlands</keywords>

<page-num>106</page-num>
<header>UIBE</header>

### THE SHAMROCK OF POWERS [170]
<note>original: Seamrag nam Buadh. While "buadh" can mean luck, it more specifically refers to inherent virtues, powers, or special attributes.</note>

Some of the people say that the four-leaved shamrock is the shamrock of luck. Others maintain that the shamrock of luck is the five-leaved shamrock. This is a very rare plant and much prized when found.

The shamrock of luck must be found, like many of the other propitious plants, "without searching, without seeking" <note>original: 'gun sireadh, gun iarraidh'</note>. When discovered in this accidental way, the lucky shamrock is warmly cherished and preserved as an invincible talisman.

"The shamrock of powers," or shamrock of luck, is often lovingly called "the shamrock of powers and of blessings" <note>original: 'seamarag nam buadh agus nam beannachd'</note>.

<image-desc>Large illuminated initial 'S' with intricate Celtic knotwork design, featuring stylized animal heads or fish within the letterform.</image-desc>

SHAMROCK of powers,
Growing beneath the bank
Whereon stood the gentle Mary,
Mother of God.

The seven felicities,
Without a shadow of misfortune,
Are upon you, O bright-moist one
Of the sunbeams—

The joy of health,
The joy of friends,
The joy of possessions,
The joy of herds,
The joy of sons, and
Of fair-white maidens,
The joy of peace,
The joy of God!

Four leaves of the straight stem,
Of the straight stem from the root of a hundred branches;
O shamrock of promise on Lady Day <note>original: La Fheill Moire; literally the Feast Day of Mary</note>,
Virtue and blessing are you at all times. [five] <note>The bracketed "five" likely refers to the five-leaved variety mentioned in the prose.</note>

<vocab>Shamrock of powers, shamrock, Mary, Mother of God, Lady Day (Feast of Mary)</vocab>

<summary>This page describes the folklore surrounding the "Shamrock of Powers," a rare four or five-leaved plant believed to bring divine blessings and prosperity if found by chance. It includes a poetic incantation invoking the Virgin Mary and listing the eight joys or "felicities" bestowed by the plant.</summary>
<keywords>Shamrock of Powers, Seamrag nam Buadh, folklore, Virgin Mary, Lady Day, talismans, Gaelic charms, botanical luck</keywords>

<page-num>107</page-num>
<header>INCANTATIONS</header>

### SHAMROCK OF LUCK

It is also called *seamarag nan each* <note>Scottish Gaelic: horse shamrock</note>, *seamarag nan searrach* <note>Scottish Gaelic: foal shamrock</note>, *seamarag an deocain* <note>Scottish Gaelic: shamrock of the deocan</note>, *seamarag an deocadain* <note>Scottish Gaelic: shamrock of the deocadan</note>, and simply, *deocan* and *deocadan*. Immediately after birth, a foal throws up a pale, soft substance resembling a sponge or the seed-cells of a codfish. This sponge-like substance coughed up by the newly-born foal is variously called *deocan, deocadan,* or *deocardan*. The people bury this in the ground, believing that the lucky shamrock grows from it, just as nettles are said to grow from human remains, whether they are buried in the pure shelly sand on the seashore or in the pure peat moss on the mountainside.

YOU shamrock of good omens,
Growing beneath the bank
Whereon stood the gracious Mary,
The Mother of God.

The seven joys are,
Without traces of evil,
Upon you, peerless one
Of the sunbeams—

Joy of health,
Joy of friends,
Joy of cattle, <note>original: "kine"</note>
Joy of sheep,
Joy of sons, and
Daughters fair,
Joy of peace,
Joy of God!

The four leaves of the straight stem, <insert>[or five]</insert>
Of the straight stem from the root of the hundred rootlets,
You shamrock of promise on Mary’s Day, <note>Likely referring to the Feast of the Annunciation or Lady Day</note>
Bounty and blessing you are at all times.

<summary>This page describes the folk belief that the "lucky shamrock" grows from biological matter expelled by newborn foals and provides a poetic incantation invoking the "seven joys" and the blessing of the Virgin Mary upon the plant.</summary>
<keywords>shamrock, folklore, botany, charms, Virgin Mary, Gaelic tradition, animal husbandry, superstitions</keywords>

<page-num>172</page-num>

### THE MOTHAN <note>The 'mothan' is generally identified as the Bog Violet or Pearlwort, believed in Highland folklore to have magical protective and aphrodisiac properties.</note>

THE 'mothan' is one of the most prized plants in the occult science of the people. It is used in promoting and conserving the happiness of the people, in securing love, in ensuring life, in bringing good, and in warding away evil.

When the 'mothan' is used as a love-philtre <note>A drink or potion supposed to arouse love or sexual desire.</note>, the woman who gives it goes upon her left knee and plucks nine roots of the plant and knots them together, forming them into a 'cuach'—a ring or wreath. The woman places the ring in the mouth of the girl for whom it is made, in the name of the King of the sun, and of the moon, and of the stars, and in the name of the Holy Three <note>The Trinity.</note>. When the girl meets her lover, or a man whom she loves and whose love she desires to secure, she puts the ring in her mouth. And should the man kiss the girl while the 'mothan' is in her mouth, he becomes henceforth her bondsman, bound to her everlastingly in cords infinitely finer than the gossamer net of the spider, and infinitely stronger than the adamant chain of the giant.

The 'mothan' is placed under women in childbirth to ensure delivery, and it is carried by wayfarers to safeguard them on their journeys. It is sewn by women into their bodice, and by men into their vest under the left arm.

An old woman in Benbecula said:—"I gave the blessed 'mothan' to red-haired Roderick, son of Ranald of Lewis, from South End, while he was on his journey to Lochmaddy to stand trial before the sheriff; and he was acquitted, even though he was as guilty of his crime as the son of a sinner." <note>original: 'Thug mi am mothan beannaichte do Ruaraidh ruadh mac Raoghail Leothasaich as a Cheann-a-deas agus e air a thuras do Loch-nam-madadh, dol ga fhiachain air bialabh an t-siorram agus fhuair e dheth ge do bha e co ciontach 's a chionta ri mac peacaich'</note>

### I WILL PLUCK THE GRACIOUS MOTHAN <note>original: UAINIDH mi am mothan suairce</note>

I will pluck the gracious 'mothan',
As the victorious King of the world plucked it;
In the name of the Father, and of the Son, and of the lasting Spirit,
With Bridget, and Mary, and Michael before me.

I am in the red, perilous battlefield,
Where all fury and anger are drained away;
The cause of all happiness and all joy,
The shield of the Lord protecting me.

<summary>This page describes the folklore surrounding the 'mothan' plant, detailing its use as a love charm, a protective amulet for travelers, and an aid in legal and childbirth matters, accompanied by a protective incantation.</summary>
<keywords>mothan, folklore, love-philtre, Benbecula, charms, protection, herbal magic, St. Bridget, St. Michael</keywords>

## THE ‘MOTHAN’ <note>original: mothan; likely the bog violet or pearlwort, believed to have magical protective properties.</note>

“...the ‘mothan’ to red Roderick, son of Ranald of Lewis, from the South-end of Uist, while he was on his journey to Lochmaddy to be tried before the sheriff. And he got off, although he was as guilty of the crime as the son of a sinner.” 

“But, Christina, why did you give the ‘mothan’ to the man when you knew that he was guilty? I think myself it was not right of you to go and do it!” 

“O, by food and clothing! <note>original: O bhith ’s aodaich!; a traditional Gaelic exclamation or oath.</note> You dear one of my heart, and you loved one of my people, I could not bring myself to refuse him. He beseeched me, and he pleaded with me, and he swore to me, and he placed a thing <note>likely a bribe or payment</note> in my hand. And oh! King of the moon, and of the sun, and of the beautiful, sublime stars, what could I myself say or do, with the poor man in his black trouble, in his red difficulty, and in his hard plight!” 

I remembered Bacon <note>likely a reference to Francis Bacon’s philosophical views on human nature or legal ethics</note> and was silent.

To drink the milk of an animal that ate the ‘mothan’ ensures immunity from harm. If a man makes a miraculous escape, it is said of him, “He drank the milk of the guileless cow that ate the ‘mothan.’” <note>original: Dh’ol e bainne na bo ba a dh’ ith am mothan.</note>

->***<-

I will pluck the gracious ‘mothan,’ <gloss>bog violet (?)</gloss>
As the victorious King of the universe plucked it;
In the name of the Father, and of the Son, and of the Spirit everlasting,
With Bride, and Mary, and Michael before me. <note>Bride (St. Bridget), Mary (the Virgin Mary), and Michael (the Archangel) are frequently invoked together in Celtic Christian charms.</note>

I am in the field of red conflict,
In which every wrath and fury are quelled,
The cause of all joy and gladness,
With the shield of the Lord protecting me.

## THE CROSS OF VIRTUES [175]

O gentle Cross of virtues,
Which the holy blood of the Lamb sanctified,
Son of mild Mary, Foster-son of Bridget of the cattle,
Son of great Mary, Mother who helps the people.

There is no tower, nor land,
There is no shower, nor ocean,
There is no pool, nor water,
There is no forest, nor mountain-side,
That is not peaceful for me,
With the help of the Cross of virtues,
That is not peaceful for me,
With the help of the Cross of virtues.

### THE FIR CLUB-MOSS [176]

### THE CHARM FOR RED WATER [180]
<note>original: Eolas a Bhun Deirg. "Red water" refers to bovine babesiosis, a parasitic disease in cattle that causes bloody urine.</note>

In performing the incantation for red water, the healer <note>original: "exorcist," though in this context it refers to a traditional folk healer or "wise woman."</note> forms her two palms into a basin. She places this basin under the urine of the cow or other animal affected, and throws the urine into water, preferably running water.

<image-desc>An ornate, illuminated capital letter 'N' with intricate Celtic knotwork. The letter is stylized with a long, curving tail that ends in a clawed foot.</image-desc>In the name of the gentle Father,
In the name of the Son of mourning,
In the name of the Holy Spirit.
->Amen.<-

Great sea, red sea,
Strength of the sea, strength of the ocean,
The nine wells of the Son of Lir <note>original: Mhic-a-Lir. Manannán mac Lir is a sea deity in Gaelic mythology.</note>,
May they bring help to you, O creature,
To put a stop to your blood,
To put a flow to your urine.
[Mention the animal's name.]

### THE STRINGLEIN

"But it is the strangles,"

## THE STRANGLES
<note>The "strangles" is a highly contagious respiratory infection in horses, characterized by abscesses in the lymph nodes of the throat.</note>

"A horse has the strangles,"

<page-num>128</page-num>
<header>CHARM</header>
<page-num>[184]</page-num>

### THE FOX’S CHARM

The fox was the plague of the people of the Highlands, killing their sheep just as the wolf killed their cattle and the polecat <note>original: "foumart"</note> killed their poultry. Due to the wildness of the land and the small, scattered population, the Highlands were the natural habitat for beasts and birds of prey and other harmful creatures, which took the people much time and effort to control.

Much could be written about the intelligence of the fox. One of the tales illustrating this cleverness is known as "The Fox of the Mull" <note>original: "Sionnach na Maoile"</note>—referring to the Mull of Kintyre. This fox never caused destruction near his own den—always traveling considerable distances to make his raids, sometimes ten or twenty miles. He caused much injury to the sheep he attacked and to the dogs that chased him. When hard-pressed, the fox would leap over a certain precipice, and the dogs would leap over after him. The dogs were always found dead on the rocks below, but never the fox, who in due time would reappear as before.

Nothing could be seen from above or below the precipice to explain how the fox survived. No shelf or ledge was visible where the fox could have landed, and the people were baffled. However, the fox-hunter was not satisfied; obtaining ropes, he climbed down the precipice and examined it carefully. He found a sapling mountain ash <note>also known as a rowan tree, often associated with protection in folklore</note> growing out of the rock, marked as if to distinguish it from the saplings of ordinary ash, bramble, sycamore <note>original: "plane"</note>, and other woods which—

<note>The following is a rhythmic incantation or "sian" intended to protect livestock from the fox. Original language: Scottish Gaelic.</note>

Let the charm of the wild-dog <note>original: "choin-choille," literally "dog of the wood," a term for the fox</note> be,
Around the paws of the fox,
Around his desire, around his stomach,
Around the throat of the greedy one,
Around the sharp, jutting tooth,
Around the hook of his entrails.

Let the charm of the Lord be around the sheep-flock,
The charm of Christ, the kindly-white, the gentle-white,
The charm of Mary, the smooth-white, the soft-white,
Against dogs, against birds, against men,
Against fairy dogs, against worldly dogs,
In this world here, and in the world beyond.

<summary>This page describes the fox as a cunning predator in the Scottish Highlands and provides a protective charm (sian) used by shepherds to safeguard their flocks from both natural and supernatural threats.</summary>
<keywords>fox, folklore, Mull of Kintyre, livestock protection, charms, Scottish Highlands, rowan tree, predators</keywords>

--- Page 149 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of folklore surrounding the fox, specifically focusing on a legendary fox from the Mull of Kintyre and the protective charms used to guard livestock against such predators.</meta>

<page-num>129</page-num>
<header>INCANTATIONS</header>

### THE SPELL OF THE FOX

...were growing in the neighborhood. And he found that by bending the marked mountain ash <note>also known as the rowan tree, often associated with protection in folklore</note> to a certain degree from its perpendicular and at a certain angle to the plane of the precipice, it touched a narrow, thread-like, winding ledge that might yield a precarious footing to a cat, to a marten, or possibly to a fox. This ledge led away to other ledges up and down the cliff. The fox-hunter cut the marked sapling, securing it, however, in its place. 

When the next havoc among the sheep had occurred, and the next pursuit of the fox had followed, the fox was found dead at the foot of the precipice with the marked mountain ash in his mouth! Choosing the tough mountain ash sapling in preference to the other less tough saplings showed sagacity; leaping from the precipice and seizing the sapling in mid-air to arrest his fall showed courage; and taking the precipice at an angle by which to get the sapling to land him in the only possible spot showed intelligence of a high order in the fox. The scene of this story has ever since been called ‘<term>Creag an t-Sionnaich</term>’ <note>Gaelic: "The Rock of the Fox"</note>—precipice of the fox. 

The conduct of this fox gave rise to many sayings among the people: 
* ‘<term>Co carrach ri sionnach ruadh Maol Chinntire</term>’—As crafty as the red fox of the Mull of Kintyre. 
* ‘<term>Co seolta ri sionnach na Maoile</term>’—As cunning as the fox of the Mull. 
* ‘<term>Co siogada sinn seanarach ri sionnach na Maoile</term>’—As ancient and wise as the great-great-grandfather fox of the Mull. 
* ‘<term>Bheir e leis a chreag sibh mar a thug an sionnach na todhlairean</term>’—He will lead you over the cliff as the fox led the hounds.

<margin>[The wolf,</margin>
May the spell of the wood-dog <note>original: "wood-dog," a poetic term for the wolf</note> be,
Upon the feet of the fox,
Upon his heart, upon his liver,
Upon his gullet of greediness,
Upon his sharp, pointed teeth,
Upon the curve of his stomach.

May the charm of the Lord be upon the sheep-kind,
The charm of Christ, kindly-white and mild-white.
The charm of Mary, lovely-fair and tender-fair.
Against dogs, against birds, against mankind,
Against fairy dogs <note>supernatural hounds from the spirit world</note>, against worldly dogs,
Of the world here, and of the world beyond.

## PRAYER OF THE CATTLE-FOLD [185] <note>original: Ora Cuithe</note>

<image-desc>A large, ornate initial capital 'U' formed by intricate Celtic knotwork and three stylized dragon or serpent heads. ## PRAYER OF THE CATTLE-FOLD

I DRIVE the cattle <note>original: "kine"</note> within
The gateway of the herds,
By the voice of the ancestors <note>original: "voice of the dead"</note>,
By the voice of the bull,
By the voice of mating <note>original: "pairing"</note>,

Like a heavy, full-weighted tether
Trailing from the curve of your haunch <note>original: "hunch of your rump"</note>,
Until bright daylight comes tomorrow.

The Father, the Son, and the Holy Spirit,
Save you, and shield you, and tend you,
Until I or mine shall meet you again.

<page-num>132</page-num>
<header>VEIN OF MARY</header>

### THE VEIN OF MARY [186] <note>original: Feith Mhoire</note>

Flat moorland is generally intersected with innumerable veins, channels, and ditches. Sometimes these are serious obstacles to cattle, more especially to cows, which are accurate judges of safety. When a cow hesitates to cross, the person driving her throws a stalk or a twig into the ditch before the unwilling animal and sings the ‘Vein of Mary’ <note>original: Feith Mhoire</note> to encourage her to cross, and to assure her that a safe bridge is before her. 

The stalk may be of any corn or grass except the reed, and the twig of any wood except the wild fig, the aspen, and the thorn. All these are forbidden, or ‘crossed’ as the people say, because of their ungracious conduct to the Gracious One <note>The Virgin Mary or Christ</note>. The reed is ‘crossed’ because it carried the sponge dipped in vinegar; the fig tree because of its inhospitality; the aspen because it held up its head haughtily, proud that the cross was made of its wood, when all the trees of the forest bowed in sorrow.

<image-desc>A large, ornate initial 'F' with intricate Celtic interlace patterns. Within the letter's loops are a bird's head and a fish.</image-desc>

Vein of Mary,
Vein of Mary;
Nimble feet,
Nimble feet;
Vein of Mary,
Vein of Mary;
Nimble feet beneath you,
A sturdy bridge before you.

Mary placed a stalk in it,
Bridget placed a hand in it,
Columba placed a foot in it,
Patrick placed a cold stone.

Vein of Mary,
Vein of Mary;
Nimble feet,
Nimble feet;
Vein of Mary,
Vein of Mary;
Nimble feet beneath you,
A sturdy bridge before you.

It also explains the folklore behind why certain plants, like the reed and aspen, are considered cursed or "crossed."</summary>
