### THE DITCH OF MARY

—all except the aspen alone—bowed their heads in reverence to the King of Glory passing by on the way to Calvary; and the thorn-tree <note>likely referring to the hawthorn</note> because of its prickly pride in having been made into a crown for the King of Kings. Notwithstanding, however, the wand of safety and the hymn of the herdsman, a cow driven against her will sometimes sinks into the ditch while crossing. This may necessitate the assistance of neighbors to extricate her from her helpless position. Hence the proverb:—<term>‘Is e fear na bo fein theid’s an fheithe an tos’</term> <note>original Gaelic: It is the owner of the cow himself who shall go into the ditch first.</note>—It is the man of the cow himself who shall go into the ditch first. The practice of throwing down the wand and repeating the hymn gave rise to a proverb among the more skeptical of the people:—<term>‘Cha dean thu feith Mhoire orm-s’ idir a mhicean’</term> <note>original Gaelic: You will not perform a 'Mary's ditch' [incantation] on me at all, my little son.</note>—You will not make a “ditch of Mary” upon me at all, sonnie.

<header>THE HIND</header>

## THE HIND [187] <note>original: AN EILID</note>

As Peter and Paul were passing by,
A hind <note>a female red deer</note> in the forest was giving birth to a calf;
"The hind is giving birth," said Peter;
"I see that she is," said Paul.

Just as the leaf falls from the tree,
So may her afterbirth <note>original: seile</note> fall to the ground,
In the name of the Father of glory and the Son of joy,
And the Spirit of loving wisdom;
The Father of glory and the Son of joy,
And the Spirit of loving wisdom.

## COLUMBA, PETER, AND PAUL [188] <note>original: CALUM-CILLE, PEADAIL, AGUS POL</note>

One day as I was going to Rome,
I met Columba <note>St. Columba, the missionary to the Scots</note>, Peter, and Paul,
And the conversation they had, and the words in their mouths,
Were of sharp-horned, female, white-bellied calves,
As was spoken in the prophecy,
On this very spot until a year and a day have passed,
By the grace of the God of the elements and of all virtues,
Lord of lords and of the eternal Powers above.

<vocab>Peter, Paul, Hind, Columba, Rome, Father, Son, Spirit, Powers</vocab>

<summary>This page features two Gaelic charms invoking Christian saints (Peter, Paul, and Columba) to ensure the safe birth of deer and the health of young cattle. These texts demonstrate the blending of folk belief with Christian liturgy in Highland tradition.</summary>
<keywords>St. Peter, St. Paul, St. Columba, deer, cattle, charms, childbirth, folk religion, Gaelic tradition</keywords>

## THE HIND

Peter and Paul were passing by,
While a hind <note>A female red deer.</note> in the path was bearing a fawn,
"A hind is bearing there," said Peter;
"I see it is so," said Paul.

"As her foliage falls from the tree,
So may her placenta <note>original: "afterbirth"</note> fall to the ground,
In the name of the Father of love and of the Son of grace,
And of the Spirit of loving wisdom;
Father of love and Son of grace,
And Spirit of loving wisdom."

## COLUMBA, PETER, AND PAUL

One day as I was going to Rome,
I met with Columba, Peter, and Paul;
The talk that they had, and that came from their mouths,
Was of loud-lunged, white-bellied, female calves,
As was spoken in the prophecy,
On this foundation for a year and a day,
Through the bosom of the God of life and all the hosts,
Chief of chiefs and of the everlasting Powers above.

## THE INDIGESTION SPELL

> The spell made by Saint Columba <note>original: "Columba"; the patron saint of the Hebrides, often invoked in charms for protecting cattle</note>,
> For the lone cow of the woman,
> Against the *crailleach* <note>original: "crailleach"; likely a specific swelling or ailment of the digestive tract</note>, against the gum disease,
> Against the bag <note>likely referring to an inflamed udder or a swelling of the stomach</note>, against the *colg* <note>original: "colg"; literally "a bristle" or "awn," referring to an internal irritation caused by swallowing sharp grain or grass</note>,
> Against the indigestion;
>
> Against the flux disease <note>original: "flux"; dysentery or severe diarrhea</note>,
> Against the cud disease <note>a condition where a cow stops chewing its cud, often a sign of serious illness</note>,
> Against the *mir* disease <note>original: "mir"; possibly a reference to a "morsel" or a blockage in the throat</note>,
> Against the *tolg* disease <note>original: "tolg"; a swelling or a puncture-like pain</note>,
> Against the surfeit <note>an illness caused by overeating or excessive richness of pasture</note>;
>
> Against the *cil* disease <note>original: "cil"; possibly a wasting disease or a back ailment</note>,
> Against the *mil* disease <note>original: "mil"; likely "milleadh," meaning "spoiling" or "blighting" by the Evil Eye</note>,
> Against the water disease <note>dropsy or edema</note>,
> Against the red disease <note>likely "red water," a parasitic disease causing bloody urine in cattle</note>,
> Against the madness;
>
> I will split the *crailleach*,
> I will split the gum disease,
> I will split the bag,
> I will split the *colg*,
> And I will kill the indigestion;
>
> I will split the flux,
> I will split the cud,
> I will split the *mir*,
> I will split the *tolg*,
> And drive away the surfeit;
>
> I will split the *cil*,
> I will split the *mil*,
> I will split the water,
> I will split the red,
> And the madness will wither away.

<page-num>140</page-num>
<header>INCANTATION</header>

### THE CHARM FOR CHEWING THE CUD [190]

This incantation is said over an animal suffering from a surfeit <note>original: "surfeit"; an illness caused by overeating or excessive drinking, often resulting in bloating or the animal "losing its cud."</note>. It is repeated three times, representing the Three Persons of the Trinity. If the surfeit is from eating too much grass or from drinking too much water, the cow or other animal [is treated with this rhyme]:

<image-desc>A decorative initial capital 'U' formed by two intertwined bird-like creatures, facing away from each other, with their wings crossed and prominent taloned feet.</image-desc>

If you ate the grass of the nine mountains,
Of the nine hills, of the nine hillocks,
If you drank the water of the nine torrents,
Of the nine streams, of the nine pools,
O wretched Little Hairy One <note>original: "Ghruaigein"; a pet name for a cow, referring to its coat.</note> of the hard stomach,
Chew, my dear, your cud,
O wretched Little Hairy One of the hard stomach,
Chew, my dear, your cud.

<summary>This page records a traditional Gaelic folk charm used to cure bloating or indigestion in livestock by encouraging the animal to resume chewing its cud through a rhythmic invocation of the number nine.</summary>
<keywords>Eolas Cnamh Chir, incantation, surfeit, veterinary folklore, cattle charms, Gaelic tradition</keywords>

## CUD CHEWING CHARM

The affected animal begins to chew its cud upon being appealed to. If the animal does not begin to chew the cud, the cause of the swelling must be sought elsewhere, and the appropriate incantation applied.

&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;If you have eaten the grass of the nine peaks <note>original: "bens"</note>,
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;Of the nine hills <note>original: "fells"</note>, of the nine hillocks,
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;If you have drunk the water of the nine falls,
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;Of the nine streams, of the nine little lakes,
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;Poor ‘<term>Gruaigein</term>’ <note>A term of endearment for a cow, likely referring to its hairy or shaggy appearance</note> of the hard belly,
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;Loved one, chew your cud.
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;Poor ‘<term>Gruaigein</term>’ of the hard belly,
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;Loved one, chew your cud.

<sig>T</sig>

<page-num>142</page-num>
### THE CHARM OF THE CHURN-DASH [191] <note>original: Eolas a Chrannachain</note>

An evil eye or an evil spirit is powerless across water, especially across a running stream or tidal water.

"Sir Eoghan Dubh Lochiall"—Black Sir Ewen Cameron of Lochiel <note>A famous 17th-century Scottish chief known for his bravery and loyalty to the Stuart cause</note>—was at feud with Mackintosh of Moy regarding lands in Lochaber. "Gormshul mhor na Moighe"—Great Gormul of Moy, the celebrated witch—wished to destroy Lochiel, the foe of her chief and of her race. But, though she nursed her wrath and pursued her course day and night, she could not accomplish her purpose, as running water lay between herself and the object of her hatred. Lochiel knew this, and, although brave to the point of recklessness, he prudently kept out of the way of the witch-woman. But on one occasion when Lochiel was returning from a conference at Inverness, Great Gormul saw him far away on the blue horizon; but, if he was far away, she was not long in reaching him:

**Gormul:** "Step on, beloved Ewen." <note>original: Ceum ann eudail Eoghain</note>
**Lochiel:** "Step on yourself, old woman, <note>original: a chailleach</note>
And if it be necessary to take the step,
A step beyond you for Ewen." <note>original: bharrachd aig Eoghan</note>

Sir Ewen Cameron was one of the bravest men in Scotland <note>original: Albain</note>, and one of the best walkers in the Highlands <note>original: Gaeldom</note>. Many a brave Englishman <note>original: Saxon man</note> he met without flinching, but this perverse woman was trying him severely, and he was anxious to be rid of her with the least delay and the least betrayal of fear. The witch-woman observed this; and the more desperately he pressed forward, the more she pressed upon him, while she herself appeared to be only making "the footstep of a cock on a gentle autumn evening when his crop is full" <note>original: cas ceum coilich feasgar fann foghair agus a sgroban lan; a Gaelic idiom for an effortless, easy pace</note>.

**Gormul:** "Step on, you beloved Ewen,
And oh! King Goileam and King Geigean! <note>Obscure figures, possibly ancient deities or spirits invoked in witchcraft</note>
Long indeed since that day!" <note>original: Is fhada fhein o’n latha sin!</note>

<summary>This page recounts a legendary pursuit of Sir Ewen Cameron of Lochiel by the witch Gormul of Moy, illustrating the folk belief that witches cannot cross running water.</summary>
<keywords>Sir Ewen Cameron of Lochiel, Gormul of Moy, Lochaber, Scottish folklore, witchcraft, running water protection, Gaelic charms</keywords>

<page-num>143</page-num>
<header>INCANTATIONS</header>

## CHARM OF THE CHURN

LOCHIEL: "Step in yourself, old woman, <note>original: chailleach; a term for an old woman, often implying a hag or witch</note>
And if the step must be taken,
A step beyond you for Ewen."

Remembering that <term>occult power</term> <note>supernatural or magical influence</note> could not operate across running water, Lochiel suddenly swerved aside to the first stream he saw and plunged into it. The witch, frustrated at the escape of the prey she had thought safe, immediately called after him:

GORMUL: "The wish of my heart to you,
Thou best-beloved of men, Lochiel."
LOCHIEL: "The wish of your heart, old woman,
Be upon yonder grey stone."

The pillared grey stone on the bank of the river to which Lochiel pointed with his sword split from top to base! Gallant courtier though he was, Sir Ewen Cameron waited to show but scant courtesy to the great Gormul of Moy.

The influence of an <term>evil spirit</term> commanded by an <term>evil mind</term> is believed to retard or wholly to prevent <term>butter</term> from forming from the <term>cream</term> in the <term>churn</term>. This evil influence was used by one woman against another in order to magically steal the butter from her neighbor’s churn and bring it to her own. This, however, could only be done if no stream ran between the two women. A fire for <term>kindling</term> carried across a stream, however small, loses its occult power and is ineffective in spiriting away milk, cream, butter, or other milk products.

The following story was told to me in 1870 by Mor Macneill, a cottar <note>a tenant farmer occupying a small cottage with a little land</note> in Glen, Barra. Sometimes the substance is spirited out of the milk, leaving nothing behind but the appearance of it. On one occasion, a household in Skye was at the <term>peat-moss</term> <note>an area where peat is cut for fuel</note> harvesting peats; no one remained at home except the housewife and a tailor who was making clothes for the father and the sons of the house.

The housewife was up in the <term>ben</term> <note>the inner or "better" room of a two-room cottage</note> churning, and the tailor was down in the <term>butt</term> <note>the outer room or kitchen area of a cottage</note> sewing. He sat on the <term>meal-girnel</term> <note>a large wooden chest for storing meal or flour</note>, cross-legged, in the manner of tailors. Presently, a neighbor woman came in and asked for a kindling for her fire. She took the kindling and went her way. When she went out, the tailor leaped down, and taking a live cinder from the fire, placed it in the <term>water-stoup</term> <note>a bucket or vessel for carrying water</note> below—

<summary>This page recounts a legend of Sir Ewen Cameron of Lochiel outwitting a witch and explains the folk belief that running water neutralizes "evil" magic used to steal butter from neighbors.</summary>
<keywords>Lochiel, Sir Ewen Cameron, Gormul of Moy, witchcraft, butter churning, folk magic, running water, Barra, Skye, Scottish folklore</keywords>

Towards evening the housewife came down in sore distress, saying—"O Mary...

<header>INCANTATIONS</header>

"By the Father and Son, am I not a deeply shamed woman? I have been churning away at this churn the livelong day until my spirit is broken and my arms are weary, and yet I have utterly failed to bring forth butter from the churn after all! O Mary! Mary, fair Mother of grace! What shall I do when the people come home? I shall never hear the end of this failed churning until the day of my death!" 

"Place your hand in the water-stoup <note>original: "water-stoup"; a basin or vessel for holding water, often holy water</note> below the dresser and see if your butter is there," said the tailor. 

And with that, the woman placed her hand in the water-stoup as directed, three successive times. Each time she brought up a large lump of butter as fresh, fair, and fragrant as beautiful buttercups in their prime. The clever tailor had counteracted the machinations of the greedy neighbor woman by placing live cinders in the water-stoup.

### THE CHARM OF THE CHURNING <note>original: UIBE; a charm or incantation, often used in a domestic or agricultural context</note>

Come, little life; come, little living one;
Come, churn, come;
Come, churn, come;
Come, cuckoo; come, gentle rain;
Come, churn, come;
Come, churn, come;
The lark comes from the sky,
And the black-headed nun <note>original: cailleag a chinn-duibh; likely referring to a reed bunting or a similar small bird</note> comes.

Come, churn, come;
Come, churn, come;
The blackbird comes, the thrush comes,
And the music comes from the fairy mound;
Come, churn, come;
Come, churn, come;
Come, wild cat,
To put an end to your hunger; <note>original: ruch; a term for a grumbling stomach or hunger</note>
Come, churn, come;
Come, churn, come.

Come, wolf, and quench your thirst;
Come, churn, come;
Come, churn, come;
Come, poor one; come, tonight:
Come, churn, come;
Come, churn, come;
Come, beggar
Of the most loathsome appearance;
Come, churn, come;
Come, churn, come;
Come, every hungry creature,
And satisfy the thirst of your body.

### INCANTATIONS

Come, you life; <note>The original text includes question marks, possibly indicating uncertainty in the transcription of the oral charm.</note> come, you breath;
Come, you churn, come;
Come, you churn, come;
Come, you cuckoo; come, you jackdaw;

Come, you churn, come;
The little lark will come from the sky,
The little old woman <note>original: "carlin," a Scots or Northern English term for an old woman or a witch-like figure</note> of the black-cap will come.

Come, you churn, come;
Come, you churn, come;
The blackbird <note>original: "merle"</note> will come, the song thrush <note>original: "mavis"</note> will come,
The music will come from the bower;
Come, you churn, come;
Come, you churn, come;
Come, you wild cat,
To ease your throat;
Come, you churn, come;
Come, you churn, come.

Come, you hound, and quench your thirst;
Come, you churn, come;
Come, you churn, come;
Come, you poor; come, you naked;
Come, you churn, come;
Come, you churn, come;
Come, you who deserve alms
And make the most distressful moan;
Come, you churn, come;
Come, you churn, come;
Come, every hungry creature,
And satisfy the thirst of your body.

<header>INCANTATIONS</header>

## THE EVIL THAT WOULD DO ME HARM [193]

This and other poems were obtained from Isabella Chisholm, a traveling tinker. Although old, Isabella Chisholm was still tall and straight, fine-featured, and fresh-complexioned. She was endowed with personal attraction, mental ability, and astute diplomacy of no common order. Her father, John Chisholm, is said to have been a "pious, prayerful man"—terms not usually applied to his class. Isabella <note>The "tinkers" or Scottish Travelers were a distinct nomadic group with a rich oral tradition of songs and spells.</note>

<image-desc>A decorative woodcut featuring a stylized Celtic knot design within a shield-like or U-shaped frame. The knotwork is intricate, forming interwoven patterns, and a small bird-like or floral element is visible at the top center.</image-desc>

The evil that would do me harm,
May it take the disease of the "gluck-glock" <note>original: "galar gluc gloc"; likely an onomatopoeic term for a choking or gurgling ailment</note>,
Pimpled, prickly, dizzying,
Swelling, festering, and gloomy.

May it be harder than a stone,
May it be blacker than the coal,
May it be swifter than the wild duck,
May it be heavier than the lead.

May it be more stinging, stinging, sharper, rougher, more piercing,
Than the hard, heart-wounding holly,
May it be more fierce than the salt—stormy, sharp, bitter, salty,
Seven times seven times over.

Tumbling over there,
Tumbling back here,
Trudging downward,
Clambering upward.

<header>INCANTATIONS</header>
<page-num>155</page-num>

## THE WICKED WHO WOULD ME HARM

Chisholm had none of the dark skin or distant gaze typical of the ordinary gypsy. However, she possessed gypsy habits and spoke the gypsy languages—variously known as "Cant," "Shelta," or "Romany"—alongside a rich, fluent command of Gaelic and English. She knew many curious spells, runes, and hymns that would have enriched Gaelic literature, as well as many rare words, phrases, and expressions that would have improved the Gaelic dictionary.

<margin><note>original: gluc gloc</note> [A gurgling or choking sound in the throat]</margin>
May the wicked person who would do me harm
Contract the throat disease <note>original: "gluc gloc," likely referring to a swelling or choking ailment</note>,
Globularly, spirally, and circularly,
With discharges, lumps, and a grim, horny texture.

May it be harder than stone.
May it be blacker than coal.
May it be swifter than a duck.
May it be heavier than lead.

May it be fiercer, sharper, harsher, and more malignant
Than the hard, wound-quivering holly wood.
May it be seven times seven times sourer
Than the consecrated <note>original: "sained"</note>, lustrous, bitter, salty salt.

Oscillating that way,
Undulating this way,
Staggering downwards,
Floundering upwards.

<page-num>158</page-num>
<header>AUGURY</header>

### THE AUGURY OF MARY
<meta>folio number 194</meta>

The *frith* <note>original: "frith"; a Gaelic term for a specific type of divination or augury</note>, or augury, was a species of divination enabling the *frithir* <note>original: "frithir"; the person performing the augury</note>, or augurer, to see into the unseen. This divination was made to ascertain the position and condition of the absent and the lost, and was applied to both humans and beasts. The augury was performed on the first Monday of the quarter, immediately before sunrise. The augurer—fasting, with bare feet, bare head, and closed eyes—went to the doorstep and placed a hand on each doorpost. Mentally beseeching the God of the unseen to show him his quest and to grant him his augury, the augurer opened his eyes and looked steadfastly straight in front of him. From the nature and position of the objects within his sight, he drew his conclusions.

<image-desc>A large, ornate initial 'S' in the style of Celtic knotwork, forming a serpentine creature with a dragon-like head at the top. The body of the 'S' is filled with intricate interlace patterns.</image-desc>
God over me, God under me,
->God before me, God behind me,<-
->I am on your path, O God,<-
->You, O God, are on my track.<-

The augury Mary made for her Son,
The offering Bride <note>St. Bridget</note> held in her palm,
"Have you seen him, O King of the elements?"—
->The King of the elements said that he had.<-

The augury of Mary for her own offspring,
When he was a time wandering,
Knowledge of truth without knowledge of lies,
->That I myself may see what I am missing.<-

May the Son of the mild-white Mary, King of the elements,
Reveal to me what I am missing,
With grace that fails not, appearing before me,
->That shall never fade nor grow dim.<-

<vocab>The Augury of Mary, augury, augurer, divination, God, Mary, Bride, King of the elements, Son of Mary</vocab>

<summary>This page describes the 'frith,' a traditional Gaelic divination ritual performed at sunrise to locate lost persons or property, accompanied by a prayer invoking Mary and St. Bride.</summary>
<keywords>frith, augury, divination, folk magic, St. Bride, Virgin Mary, Gaelic folklore, ritual prayer</keywords>

--- Page 179 ---
<meta>This page introduces the concept of "Frith Mhoire" (The Augury of Mary), a traditional Gaelic divination practice used to find lost people or predict the future.</meta>

<page-num>159</page-num>
### THE AUGURY OF MARY

Many men in the Highlands and Islands were famous augurers <note>original: "augurers"; practitioners of divination who predict the future or discover hidden knowledge</note>, and many stories—realistic, romantic, and extremely curious—are still told of their divinations. The people say that the Virgin Mary performed an augury when Christ was missing, and that it was by means of this divination that Mary and Joseph discovered that Christ was in the Temple debating with the teachers of the law. For this reason, this method of divination is called *‘frith Mhoire’*—the augury of Mary; and *‘frithireachd Mhoire’*—the performing of Mary’s augury.

The *‘frith’* of the Celt is related to the *‘frett’* of the Norseman. It is probable that the surnames Freer and Frere are variations of *‘frithir,’* meaning an augurer. Ancestors of people bearing this name served as astrologers to the kings of Scotland.

->God be over me, God be under me,<-
->God be before me, God be behind me,<-
->I am on Your path, O God,<-
->You, O God, are in my steps.<-

->The augury Mary made for her Son,<-
->The offering Bride <note>St. Bridget, often associated with the Virgin Mary in Gaelic tradition</note> made through her palm,<-
->Did You see it, King of life?—<-
->The King of life said that He saw.<-

->The augury made by Mary for her own child<-
->When He was missing for a time,<-
->Grant me knowledge of truth, not knowledge of falsehood,<-
->That I shall truly see the object of my quest.<-

->Son of beautiful Mary, King of life,<-
->Give me eyes to see all that I seek,<-
->With grace before me that shall never fail,<-
->And shall never be extinguished nor dimmed.<-

<summary>This page describes the "Augury of Mary," a traditional Gaelic divination practice rooted in the biblical story of finding the young Jesus in the Temple, accompanied by a prayer for divine guidance.</summary>
<keywords>Augury, Divination, Frith Mhoire, Highlands and Islands, Virgin Mary, St. Bride, Folklore, Scottish Surnames, Astrology</keywords>

<header>MISCELLANY</header>

## THE FIRST MIRACLE OF CHRIST <note>original: CIAD MIARAIL CHRIOSD</note> [195]

This poem was obtained in 1891 from Malcolm Macmillan, a crofter in Grimnis, Benbecula. Macmillan was then an old man. He heard this and many other poems when he was a boy from old people who, when evicted from Uist <note>referring to the Highland Clearances</note>, emigrated to Prince Edward Island, Nova Scotia, Cape Breton, and other parts of North America.

<image-desc>Large ornate initial 'H' with intricate Celtic knotwork design, forming the first letter of the poem.</image-desc>Joseph and Mary went,
To be numbered in the census,
And the birds began their joyful singing
In the woods of the cuckoos.

The two were traveling the way,
Until they reached a thick forest,
And in the forest there was fruit,
As red as the strawberries.

That was the time when she was pregnant,
While she was carrying the King of Grace,
And she conceived a longing for the fruit,
That grew on the blessed slopes.

And Mary spoke to Joseph,
With a gentle, mild voice,
"Give me some fruit, Joseph,
So that I may satisfy my craving."

And Joseph spoke to Mary,
With a hard pain in his heart,
"I will give you fruit, Mary,
But who is the father of your burden?" <note>original: "co is athair dha d' throm"; "burden" refers to her pregnancy.</note>

<summary>This page introduces a traditional Gaelic poem titled "The First Miracle of Christ," a folk version of the Cherry Tree Carol collected from a crofter in Benbecula. The verses describe Joseph and Mary traveling to the census and Joseph's initial doubt regarding Mary's pregnancy when she asks for fruit.</summary>

## THE VIRGIN AND THE CHILD [196]
<note>original: AN OIGH AGUS AN LEANABH</note>
THE Virgin was seen approaching,
With Christ as a young child in her arms,
Angels were bowing in obedience to them,
The King of the elements <note>original: Righ nan dul; a common Celtic title for God as ruler of the natural world</note> declaring it was right.

The Virgin with hair like golden dew,
The Jesus who is whiter than the snow,
Musical seraphim singing their praise,
The King of the elements declaring it was right.

## GOD OF THE MOON [197]
<note>original: DIA NA GILE</note>
GOD of the moon, God of the sun,
God of the globe, God of the stars,
God of the floods, the land, and the heavens,
Who ordained for us the King of generosity.

It was the gentle Mary who went upon her knee,
It was the Being of the elements who went into her lap,
Darkness and sadness were put away,
And the star of guidance rose early on high.

The land was revealed, the soil was revealed,
The mountain-seas and the streams were revealed,
Sorrow was laid low and joy was raised up,
Music was established with harp and with lute.

<summary>Two devotional poems celebrating the birth of Christ, emphasizing the Virgin Mary's role and God's sovereignty over the natural elements and celestial bodies.</summary>

### THE VIRGIN AND CHILD

The Virgin was seen approaching,
With Christ so young upon her breast,
Angels offered them their humble service,
While the King of Glory <note>Referring to God the Father</note> declared it is just.

The Virgin with hair like gold-dewed locks,
The Jesus whiter than the fallen snow,
Seraphs <note>The highest order of angels</note> of song singing their praise,
While the King of Glory declared it is just.

### GOD OF THE MOON

God of the moon, God of the sun,
God of the globe, God of the stars,
God of the waters, the land, and the skies,
Who ordained for us the King of Promise,

It was fair Mary who knelt upon her knee,
It was the King of Life who rested in her lap,
Darkness and tears were left behind,
And the star of guidance <note>The Star of Bethlehem</note> rose early.

It illuminated the land, it illuminated the world.
It illuminated the still waters and the rushing currents,
Grief was laid to rest and joy was raised up.
Music was established with the small harp and the pedal-harp. <note>original: "harp and pedal-harp" likely referring to different sizes or styles of the instrument used in traditional worship.</note>

## GOD OF THE MOON, GOD OF THE SUN <meta>Poem number 198</meta>
<image-desc>Large ornate initial 'I' with intricate Celtic knotwork design, forming part of the first line of the poem.</image-desc>
God of the moon, God of the sun,
Who ordained for us the Son of meekness.
The smooth white Mary on her knee,
Christ, King of the elements, in her arms.
I am the cleric of the little peaks,
Going around the steep, craggy rocks.
I can see the hill, I can see the shore,
I can see angels floating on the waves,
I can see a shapely, rounded leg <note>This likely refers to a vision of a divine or angelic figure approaching</note>,
Coming to land with friendship for us.

## THE SECURITY OF THE GENEROUS <meta>Poem number 199</meta>
This verse, the only verse of the poem he could remember, was obtained from John Kane, a native of Ireland. John Kane had many traditional stories of Saint Columba showing that he "being dead yet speaks." <note>A reference to Hebrews 11:4</note> These stories were:

Columba <note>original: Calum-cille</note> says to us,
That the generous shall never go to hell;
But the people of theft and the people of false oaths,
They shall lose their right to God.

## GOD OF THE MOON, GOD OF THE SUN

    God of the moon, God of the sun,
    Who ordained for us the Son of mercy.
    The fair Mary upon her knee,
    Christ the King of life in her lap.
    I am the established cleric,
    Going around the foundation stones,
    I behold mansions, I behold shores,
    I behold angels floating,
    I behold the shapely rounded column
    Coming landwards in friendship to us.

## SAFETY OF THE GENEROUS

<margin>The style is vivid and graphic; the probable and improbable, the possible and impossible, blend and diffuse throughout the text.</margin>

    COLUMBA <note>St. Columba (521–597 AD), an influential Irish abbot and missionary who spread Christianity to Scotland.</note> tells us that
    To hell the generous shall never go:
    But those who steal and those who swear <note>original: "swear" in this context likely refers to perjury or taking the Lord's name in vain.</note>,
    They shall lose their right to God.

### A MOTHER’S CONSECRATION [200] <note>original: COISTRIG MATHAR</note>

THE following lines are whispered by mothers into the ears of sons and daughters when leaving their homes in the Outer Isles for the towns of the south and for foreign lands.

<image-desc>A large, ornate initial 'N' decorated with intricate Celtic knotwork and an animal head (resembling a dog or wolf) at the top, facing left. The 'N' forms a decorative border down the left side of the first few lines of the text.</image-desc>

May the great God be between your two shoulders,
To protect you in going and in returning;
May the Son of Mary the Virgin be near your heart,
And may the perfect Spirit be poured out upon you—
Oh, may the perfect Spirit be poured out upon you!

[Taskill <note>original: Thascail</note> [Health/Slàinte <note>original: Shlainte; likely used here as a name or a closing wish for well-being</note>

### THE MOTHER'S CONSECRATION

It is likely that the last sounds to linger in the ears of a Gael, as they sink into the sleep of death, are the final echoes of a mother's voice—heard long ago in a distant home among the mist-covered hills or on the coastal plains <note>original: "machair"; fertile low-lying grassy plain</note> washed by the sea.

MAY the great God be between your two shoulders
To protect you in your going and in your coming;
May the Son of the Virgin Mary be near your heart,
And may the perfect Spirit be poured upon you—
Oh, may the perfect Spirit be poured upon you!

### THE CRUCIFIED MAN <page-num>201</page-num>

<image-desc>A large decorative initial 'R' with intricate Celtic knotwork design, extending vertically down the left margin.</image-desc>
The two following poems were obtained in Kintail. They are obscure in themselves, and the dialect of Kintail in which they were recited <note>The editor notes that the local dialect makes the transcription difficult.</note>

O Man who was hung upon the tree,
<margin>sentence</margin> O Man who was tortured by the will of the people,
Now that I have grown old and gray,
Take pity, O God, on my confession!

It is no wonder to me that my sin is great,
As I am a poor, babbling, noisy fellow;
In my youth I was foolish,
In my old age I am wretched.

A while before the Son of God came,
The universe was a black, miry pool,
Without king, without prosperity, without a moon,
Without a fold, without a body, without a form.

The land shone, the ground shone,
The heavy, gray ocean shone,
The entire universe shone,
In the era of the Son of God’s coming to the house <note>original: "tigh'nn gu teach" — likely meaning His incarnation or coming to dwell among men.</note>.

That was when Mary of the graces spoke,
The kindly Virgin who was always wise,
When Joseph gave her his love,
It was his desire to be in her presence often.

<summary>This page introduces two religious poems collected from Kintail, beginning with a repentant prayer and a description of the world's transformation through the birth of Christ.</summary>
<keywords>Kintail, Crucifixion, Confession, Creation, Virgin Mary, Joseph, Gaelic Poetry</keywords>

# HE WHO WAS CRUCIFIED

The way these were recited increases their obscurity. The reciters repeated them as one poem, but were uncertain whether they were one or two poems.

&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;You who were hanged upon the tree,
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;And were crucified by the condemnation of the people.
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;Now that I am grown old and gray,
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;Receive my confession-prayer; O God, have pity!

&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;It is no wonder to me that my wickedness is great,
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;I am but a poor, clattering cymbal <note>original: "clattering cymbal," a biblical allusion to 1 Corinthians 13:1, signifying empty noise without love</note>.
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;In my youth I was profane,
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;In my old age I am forlorn.

&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;In the time before the Son of God came,
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;The earth was a black morass <note>original: "morass," a tract of soft, wet ground or a marsh</note>,
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;Without star, without sun, without moon,
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;Without body, without heart, without form.

&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;He illuminated the plains, illuminated the hills,
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;He illuminated the great green sea;
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;He illuminated the whole globe together,
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;When the Son of God came to earth.

&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;Then it was that the Mary of grace spoke,
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;The Virgin who is always most kindly and wise;
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;When Joseph gave to her his love,
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;He desired to be often in her presence.

<page-num>174</page-num>

# MISCELLANY <note>original: MEASGAIN</note>

There was a covenant between Joseph <note>original: Eos</note> and the Virgin <note>original: Oigh</note>,
In a lawful and proper order,
That the matter would be settled
With the seal of the Great King of powers.

They went with it to the Temple of God,
Where the clergy were within;
As the Great High King ordained,
They married before they came out.

An angel came afterward:—
"Joseph, what is this mood you are in?"
"I received a woman from the clergy,
It is not natural for me to be at peace."

"Joseph, stay with your spouse,
It is not fitting for you to speak of fault;
For what you have is the pure Virgin,
Upon whom no man has laid a hand."

"How shall I believe that from you?
I myself, alas, have knowledge—
When I lay down by her shoulder,
A living child was stirring under her girdle."

<summary>This poem recounts the traditional story of Joseph's doubt regarding Mary's pregnancy following their marriage in the Temple, and the angel's intervention to reassure him of her purity.</summary>
<keywords>Joseph, Virgin Mary, Marriage, Temple of God, Angel, Virgin Birth, Gaelic Folklore</keywords>

### MISCELLANEOUS

A compact <note>original: "compact"; here meaning a formal agreement or betrothal</note> there was between Joseph and the Virgin,
In an order well-becoming and just,
So that the agreement might be confirmed
By the seal of the Great King of laws. <note>Referring to God's divine sanction of the union.</note>

They went with him to the Temple of God,
Where the clerics <note>original: "clerics"; referring to the priests or temple officials</note> sat within:
As ordained by the Great High King,
They married before they came out.

An angel came afterwards:—
“Joseph, why are you so agitated?”
“I received a woman from the clerics;
It is not natural for me to be calm.”

“Joseph, abide by your reason;
It is not enlightened of you to find fault.
What you have received is a pure virgin,
On whom man never laid a hand.”

“How can I believe that from you?
I myself—to my grief!—have knowledge:
When I laid myself down by her shoulder,
A living child throbbed beneath her girdle.” <note>original: "girdle"; a belt or sash, here referring to the womb or waistline.</note>

<summary>This text recounts a traditional narrative of the marriage between Joseph and Mary at the Temple, followed by Joseph's distress and an angel's reassurance regarding the miraculous nature of Mary's pregnancy.</summary>

### THAT ROOSTER

<note>This poem recounts a folk legend often associated with the Resurrection or the story of St. Stephen, where a cooked rooster miraculously comes to life to testify to the truth.</note>

**I**t was then the fierce woman spoke—
"It is the wicked ones who have caused my ruin;
Put the liar down under the ground,
And your life will be better in my house.

That rooster you have in the pot,
Pounded as soft as cabbage,
The liar <note>original: "breugadair"; likely referring to a person accused of lying about a miracle</note> shall not go into the grave
Until the rooster crows on the beam."

The rooster went upon the beam,
He tucked his two wings to his body;
He crowed there most tastefully and sweetly,
And my King <note>referring to Christ</note> came from the tree <note>original: "chroibh"; the Cross</note>.

Those people are not pleasing to God—
The folk of lies and the folk of false oaths;
He preferred the true prayer
And the flood of tears running fast from the eyes.

<vocab>Rooster, liar, beam/rafter, King, God, prayer</vocab>

<summary>This poem recounts a miracle where a cooked rooster returns to life and crows from a rafter to prove the truth of a divine event, contrasting the wickedness of liars with the virtue of sincere prayer.</summary>
<keywords>miracle, rooster, folklore, Christ, truth vs. lies, Gaelic poetry</keywords>

<header>MISCELLANEOUS</header>

## THE COCK

<note>This poem refers to a widespread folk legend where a cooked cock (rooster) rises from a pot to crow, proving the truth of Christ's resurrection or the innocence of a victim.</note>

It was then the angry woman spoke—
“It was the wicked who caused my ruin;
Drive the liar down below the beam,
And you shall be welcome in my house.

That cock you have in the pot,
Chopped as small as the kale <note>original: "kail," a type of cabbage or greens</note>,
The liar shall not go to the pit <note>The "pit" refers to hell or a place of punishment.</note>
Until that cock shall crow upon the spar <note>original: "spar," a wooden beam or rafter.</note>.”

The cock went up upon the spar,
He pressed his two wings to his body,
He crew sweetly and melodiously,
And my King <note>Referring to Christ.</note> came from the tree <note>The "tree" is a common poetic term for the Cross.</note>.

The people not liked by God
Are those who lie and those who swear;
Rather would He have a genuine prayer
And water flowing swiftly from the eyelids <note>A metaphor for tears of true repentance.</note>.

<vocab>cock, angry woman, liar, kale, spar, King, God, prayer</vocab>

<summary>This poem recounts a miraculous legend where a cooked cock rises from a pot to crow upon a rafter, serving as a divine sign against a liar and celebrating the triumph of Christ. It concludes with a moral warning against lying and an exhortation toward sincere, tearful repentance.</summary>
<keywords>folklore, miracle of the cock, Christ, resurrection, repentance, morality, kale, rafters</keywords>

## MISCELLANY

### OMENS <note>original: Manaidh</note>

The people believed in omens of birds and beasts, fishes and insects, and of men and women. These omens were innumerable, and a few only can be mentioned. The fisher would deem it a bad omen to meet a red-haired woman when on his way to fish; and were the woman defective in mind or body, probably the man would return home muttering strong adjectives beneath his breath. On the other hand,

<image-desc>A stylized symmetrical drawing of two birds, possibly swans or geese, facing away from each other but joined at their bodies and feet. Their long, curving necks rise upwards and then curve inwards, with their heads facing outwards. Their bodies are textured with a scale-like or feather-like pattern. Their legs and feet are intertwined at the base, forming an ornate, almost floral design.</image-desc>

->On a Monday morning,<-
->I heard the bleating of a lamb,<-

->And the cry of a solitary kid<-
->While I sat quietly, stooped over,<-

->And the blue-grey cuckoo,<-
->Without food in my stomach.<- <note>To hear the cuckoo before breakfast was considered a very poor omen for the coming year.</note>

->On a Tuesday evening,<-
->I saw on a smooth flagstone,<-
->A slim, white snail,<-

->And the white wheatear<- <note>original: clacharan fionn; a small migratory bird.</note>
->On the top of a loose-stone wall,<-

->The foal of an old mare<-
->Sprawling with its back to me.<-

->I knew then, accordingly <insert>[according to those signs]</insert>,<-
->That the year would not go well for me.<-

<summary>This page describes various traditional Gaelic omens involving animals and timing, specifically detailing a series of unlucky encounters that foretell a difficult year. It highlights the cultural significance of first sightings and sounds, such as hearing a cuckoo while fasting or seeing a foal's back.</summary>

<keywords>omens, folklore, Gaelic traditions, superstitions, birds, Monday morning, Tuesday evening, cuckoo, wheatear, animal symbolism</keywords>

<header>MISCELLANEOUS</header>
<page-num>179</page-num>

## OMENS

It was considered lucky for a girl to find the red hair of a woman in the nest of certain birds, particularly in the nest of the wheatear <note>The wheatear is a small migratory bird common in rocky areas of the Highlands</note>.

<term>The red hair of a woman,</term> <note>original: Gruag ruadh boirionnaich</note>
<term>The grey beard of a man,</term> <note>original: Fiasag liath fiorionnaich</note>
<term>Are love and luck to the sloven</term> <note>original: Ruth agus rath na leirist</note>
<term>Who finds them in the nest of the wheatear.</term> <gloss>[or the titmouse]</gloss> <note>original: Gheobh an nead a chlacharain [bhigirein]</note>

Early on the morning of Monday,
I heard the bleating of a lamb,

And the kid-like cry of the snipe,
While I was sitting gently bent over,

And I heard the grey-blue cuckoo,
When I had no food in my stomach <note>Hearing the cuckoo before breakfast was traditionally considered an omen of bad luck or hunger for the coming year</note>.

On the fair evening of Tuesday,
I saw upon the smooth stone,
A snail, slimy and pale,

And the ashy-colored wheatear,
On the top of a stone wall full of holes,

And the foal of the old mare,
With a clumsy gait and its back turned toward me.

And I knew from these signs, [according to the tradition]
That the year would not go well for me.

<summary>This page records traditional Gaelic omens and rhymes concerning luck, specifically focusing on bird nests and the specific animals one encounters at the start of the week.</summary>
<keywords>omens, folklore, wheatear, cuckoo, Gaelic rhymes, luck, animal signs, Highland traditions</keywords>

<page-num>180</page-num>
<header>MISCELLANY <note>original: MEASGAIN</note></header>
<page-num>204</page-num>

### EARLY ON EASTER MONDAY <note>original: MOCH LA LUAN CASG</note>

<image-desc>A decorative woodcut illustration featuring two stylized birds, possibly eagles or falcons, facing away from each other. Their bodies are intertwined, with their wings crossed over their chests and their taloned feet visible at the bottom.</image-desc>

Early on Easter Monday,
I saw upon the sea,
A wild duck and a white swan
Swimming together.

I heard on Tuesday
The little bird of the seasons <note>original: Eunarag nan trath; likely referring to the common snipe</note>,
Bleating in the heights
And calling out.

On Wednesday I was
Gathering the crested seaweed <note>original: feamain-chir</note>,
And I saw the three <note>referring to the duck, swan, and bird mentioned previously</note>
Rising up.

I recognized at once,
That the migration <note>original: imirig</note> had begun,
And a blessing would not be there
After that.

May the protection of Bridget be,
The protection of gentle Mary,
The protection of valiant Michael,
Upon myself and my dear ones <note>original: eudail; literally "cattle" or "wealth," used as a term of endearment for family</note>.
Upon myself and my dear ones.

<summary>A rhythmic Gaelic poem or charm observing the signs of spring migration on the days following Easter and invoking the protection of the saints.</summary>
<keywords>Easter Monday, migration, nature observations, St. Bridget, Virgin Mary, St. Michael, protective charms, Gaelic folklore</keywords>

<header>MISCELLANEOUS</header>
<page-num>181</page-num>

## EARLY EASTER MONDAY

EARLY on the day of Easter Monday,
I saw on the salt water <note>original: "brine"</note>
A duck and a white swan
Swimming together.

I heard on Tuesday
The snipe of the seasons,
Bleating on high
And calling.

On Wednesday I had been
Cutting the channelled seaweed <note>original: "fucus"; a type of rockweed or brown algae</note>,
And then I saw the three
Arising.

I knew immediately
That a departure <note>original: "flitting"; often implying a supernatural or ominous moving away</note> was occurring,
And a blessing there would not be
After that.

The protection <note>original: "girth"; meaning a sanctuary, enclosure, or spiritual safeguard</note> of Bride <note>St. Bridget</note> the calm,
The protection of Mary the mild,
The protection of Michael the strong,
Be upon me and mine,
Upon me and mine.

<meta>VOLUME II.</meta>
<sig>2 A</sig>

<summary>A rhythmic poem or charm detailing various natural omens observed during the days following Easter, concluding with a prayer for protection from the saints. It reflects the traditional Gaelic integration of seasonal wildlife observations with spiritual folk beliefs.</summary>
<keywords>Easter Monday, omens, St. Bridget, Virgin Mary, St. Michael, nature poetry, Gaelic folklore, protection charm</keywords>
